[PAGE_5]
P
REFACE
This Solutions Manual for the textbook An Introduction to Technical Problem
Solving with Matlab v. 7, Sticklen and Eskil, Great Lakes Press, Inc., ISBN 1-
881018-80-6. The Solutions Manual should be available to instructors only;
instructors are asked in the interest of all instructors to restrict access to this
book.
Problems in Chapters 3 to Chapter 8 at the end of each chapter are grouped
into two sections. “Nuts and Bolts …” sections focus on the mechanics of the
MATLAB capabilities of the chapter. The problems in “Nuts and Bolts …” are
straightforward and should be considered exercises rather than full blown prob-
lems in that these problems are typically of the “turn the crank” variety. They
should be assigned to allow students to exercise their base understanding of MAT-
LAB capabilities.
The second grouping of problems at chapter ends are labeled “Problem Solv-
ing With …” These problems are aimed at giving students practice in applying the
tools of MATLAB and may more accurately be termed problems in that students
must determine what MATLAB capabilities should be applied in a given problem.
In this Solutions Manual problems from the main text are reproduced, then a
solution is given. The problem appears basically exactly the way it does in the
main text. The solution is set off between heavy bold, dashed lines, and the font of
the answer is written in a bold, monospaced font.
In many cases, the problem solution will include notes on what students
should “get out of” the problem, what instructors might assign as extensions to
problems, and in some cases, alternative solutions to problems. In using solutions
offered here, it is important that instructors realize there may be more than one
way to solve given problems.
Questions, suggestions, and comments should be sent to Mr. John Gruender,
Great Lakes Press, jg@glpbooks.com.

[PAGE_6]

S C 3
OLUTIONS FOR HAPTER
MATLAB B : S
ASICS CALARS
Problems in this chapter are solved by students without the ability to save their
work in M files (functions/scripts) since saving MATLAB work is the topic of
Chapter 4. In most cases students should be working with both the command win-
dow (to write and execute code lines) and the history window (to “re-run” code
lines). To effectively work the back and forth between the command window and
history window, students are (should be …) forced to start using MATLAB vari-
ables effectively.
Much of the what goes on in this chapter for students focuses on getting used
to the MATLAB environment in the context of performing operations that are
familiar: scalar operations.
Set A: Nuts and Bolts Problems for Scalars
Problem 3-A.1
Define a variable named angle to be 45o. Convert angle to radians using
the following:
radians = degrees   ---  ------ 
180
Store the result in the variable angleRad. Use the predefined variable pi in
your calculation.
Using the History Window, repeat the calculation for angle = 60o, 75o, and
90o.

[PAGE_8]
3–2 MATLAB Basics: Scalars
% PROBLEM 3-A.1
clc, clear
angle = 45 % degrees
angleRad = angle * (pi/180) % radians
% student then resets angle, and runs the line setting
% angleRad from the history window
Problem 3-A.2
Suppose we have: an independent variable x and two dependent variables y
and z. Here are the expressions to calculate y and z:
y = x–5
2
z = 3y –16y+74
Set x to be 5. Calculate the corresponding values of y and z.
Using the Command History Window, repeat the calculations for x = 7, 9, and
11.
This problem is a vehicle to refresh students on the concept of
dependent/independent variable – along with the continuing story
of scalar operations and using the command and history windows.
% PROBLEM 3-A.2
clc, clear
x = 5 % indep var
y = x - 5 % dep var
z = 3*y^2 - 16*y + 74 % dep var
% student resets x, then recomputes y and z using the
% history window

[PAGE_9]
MATLAB Basics: Scalars 3–3
Problem 3-A.3
The area and the perimeter of a circle of radius r are found with the following
expressions:
2
circleArea = r
circlePerimeter = 2r
Set the radius (r) to the value 5. Calculate the area and perimeter of a circle by
entering the formulae above.
Now set the radius to be 7. Double-click on line commands in the Command
History Window to recalculate the area and perimeter.
Set the radius to 9. This time, select both line commands by holding the Ctrl
button and copy them to the Command Window. Press Enter to carry out both
calculations.
% PROBLEM 3-A.3
clc, clear
r = 5 % linear unit
circleArea = pi*r^2 % corresponding area unit
circlePerimeter = 2*pi*r % same linear unit
% student resets r, then recomputes using history window
Problem 3-A.4
What is a scalar? How does a scalar differ from a vector? How does it differ
with an array (or a matrix)? Explain in scientific and MATLAB terms.
A scalar in MATLAB is a simple number; one number. A vector in
MATLAB is a linear (1-D) group of numbers. An array in MATLAB is
a table (2-D) of numbers. In science and engineering theory, a
vector is an entity that has both magnitude and direction, like
velocity. The corresponding scalar to the velocity vector is
called speed. There is a table like entity in science and

[PAGE_10]
3–4 MATLAB Basics: Scalars
engineering theory called a tensor, but that is beyond the scope
of this book.
Problem 3-A.5
Having a magnitude and a direction, force is represented by vectors in science.
Suppose we represented a force vector in the Command Window by defining
its x and y components:
>> forceX = 3
>> forceY = 5
What type of MATLAB variables are forceX and forceY? Review the defini-
tions of scalars, vectors and arrays in Section 3-2 to verify your answer.
Both forceX and forceY are scalars.
Problem 3-A.6
Using angleRad you defined in Problem 3-A.1, find the cosine and sine of
angle. Store the results in variables cosAngle and sinAngle, respec-
tively.
% PROBLEM 3-A.6
clc, clear
angle = 45 % degrees
angleRad = angle * (pi/180) % radians
cosAngle = cos(angleRad)
sinAngle = sin(angleRad)

[PAGE_11]
MATLAB Basics: Scalars 3–5
Problem 3-A.7
Using variables cosAngle and sinAngle (Problem 3-A.6), define the fol-
lowing:
 = 45
2 2
z = cos + sin
A well-known identity in trigonometry is that the sum of cosine-squared and
sine-squared of an angle always equals one. If your result for z was not one,
review Section 3-3 and redo the operations.
% PROBLEM 3-A.7
clc, clear
alpha = 45; % degrees
angleRad = alpha * (pi/180) % radians
cosAngle = cos(angleRad)
sinAngle = sin(angleRad)
z = cosAngle^2 + sinAngle^2
Problem 3-A.8
Redefine the value of variable angle (Problem 3-A.6) as 65o and recalculate
the value of z (Problem 3-A.7) using the Command History Window.
This is the standard operation for using the history window to
“store” solutions that can be re-run.
Problem 3-A.9
Create a variable named z and set it to seven. Calculate 4z and store the result
in z.

[PAGE_12]
3–6 MATLAB Basics: Scalars
This exercise is very simple on the mechanical level. But the
point here is that using the assignment operator in MATLAB is
definitely not the same thing as equality in algebra.
% PROBLEM 3-A.9
clc, clear
z = 7
z = 4*z

Hint: Remember, ‘=’ is the assignment operator in MATLAB.
Part A >> x + 2 = 4
Part B >> x = 4 - 2
Part C >> x = 4 - (-2)
Part D >> 5 = x
Part E >> 5 + 3 = 7 + 1
Part A: not valid because left hand side is not a variable (a
container
Part B: valid because left hand side is a variable
Part C: valid because left hand side is a variable
Part D: not valid because left hand side is not a variable
Part E: not valid because left hand side is not a variable

>> x = 4
>> y = x
>> x = 6
Part B What does x contain after executing the following lines?
>> x = 2
>> x^2
Part C What does x contain after executing the following lines?
>> x = 2
>> x = 2 * x + 5
>> x = (x-3)/3
Part A: x contains 6, y contains 4
Part B: x contains 2
Part C: x contains 2

Problem 3-A.13
One way to test for the sign of scalar x is to check if x 2 equals x. Assume the
calculation works:
2
x = x
If so, then we can conclude that x is non-negative; otherwise x is negative. (A
non-negative number is one that is positive or zero. The relationship above is
NOT a MATLAB assignment statement.)
Successively set x to each of the following numbers and use the MATLAB
relational equality operator to test whether x is non–negative: {-5, 10,
35, -292, 0, 101}.
% PROBLEM 3-A.13
clc, clear
x = -5
x == sqrt(x^2)
% now reset x=10, and "run" the second line from the history
% window. Then reset x=35, and etc

[PAGE_15]
MATLAB Basics: Scalars 3–9
Problem 3-A.14
One way to check if a scalar is an integer is to round the scalar and compare
the result with itself.
The built-in MATLAB function round can be used for rounding a number.
For instance, round(5) and round(5.3) will return 5. But
round(5.6) will return 6.
Using the built-in function round and MATLAB relational operators, test if
x is an integer for the following values of x: {-5.2, 10, 35.3, -292,
0, 101.99}.
This problem is very similar to the problem of testing for a
negative number (Problem 3-A.13).
% PROBLEM 3-A.14
clc, clear
x = -5.2
x == round(x) % if x is integer, test is TRUE
% now reset x=10, and run the test again... etc
Problem 3-A.15
(Be sure you have solved Problem 3-A.13 and Problem 3-A.14 before
attempting this problem.)
Use relational and logical operators to do the following tests in MATLAB:
Part A Is 5 a non-negative integer?
Part B Is 7.7 a non-negative integer?
Part C Is 7.7 not a negative integer?
Part D Is 5 or 7.7 a negative integer?
% PROBLEM 3-A.15

[PAGE_16]
3–10 MATLAB Basics: Scalars
% Part A
clc, clear
x = 5
x == sqrt(x^2) & x == round(x)
% Part B
clc, clear
x = 7.7
x == sqrt(x^2) & x == round(x)
% Part C
clc, clear
x = 7.7
~( x ~= sqrt(x^2) & x == round(x) )
% Part D
clc, clear
x = 5
y = 7.7
( x ~= sqrt(x^2) & x == round(x) ) | ...
( y ~= sqrt(y^2) & y == round(y) )
Problem 3-A.16
Create expressions using variables x and y to answer the following questions.
So you have explicit values for x and y to test your work, set x=7 and y=5.
Part A Is x greater than 6 and less than 10? Here is another way to
ask the same question: Is x in the range (6,10)?
Part B Is x greater than or equal to 10, or less than or equal to 6?
Here is another way to ask the same question: Is x not in the
range (6, 10)?
Part C Is (x is greater than 6) and (y is greater than x but less than
10)?
Part D Is (x is in the range (6,10)) but (y not in the range)?
Part E Is x or y in the range (6,10)?
A very common beginning MATLAB programming error is to represent
a test on the range of a variable incorrectly. For example, if

[PAGE_17]
MATLAB Basics: Scalars 3–11
we want to ask if x is in the range (2,4), then the common error
is to write
2 < x < 4
This relational expression will always return 1: if 2 < x is
TRUE then the second test is 1 < 4, while if 2 < x is FALSE then
the second test is 0 < 4. In both cases the result is TRUE.
% PROBLEM 3-A.16
clc, clear
x = 7
y = 5
% Part A
(6 < x) & (x < 10)
% Part B
(6 >= x) | (x >= 10)
% alternative...
% ~( (6 < x) & (x < 10) )
% Part C
(x > 6) & (y > x) & (y < 10)
% Part D
((6 < x) & (x < 10)) & ~((6 < y) & (y < 10))
% Part E
((6 < x) & (x < 10)) | ((6 < y) & (y < 10))

Problem 3-B.1
A quadratic polynomial such as
y vertex
y = 3x 2 +2x–6 defines a parab-
ola in the two-dimensional space.
The vertex of a parabola is the
point at the top if the curve is con-
cave down or the point at the bot- x 1 x 2
tom if the curve is concave up.
x
For a quadratic polynomial
y = ax 2 +bx+c, the x coordinate
of the vertex and the peak value of
the parabola can be found using
this relationship:
b
x = –------
vertex 2a
2
y = ax +bx +c
vertex vertex vertex
In the Command Window, set the values of the constants to be a=1, b=3, and
c=2. Calculate the value of x . Calculate y .
vertex vertex
Set b=4. Recalculate the values of x and y by selecting the
vertex vertex
appropriate command lines in the History Window and carrying them to the
Command Window. Note that you do not need to redefine variables a and c in
this case since they are already set to their original values.
Set c=4 and recalculate x and y . For which value of b did you
vertex vertex
calculate the vertex coordinates, 3 or 4?
This problem is again an exercise in using the history window to
“save” commands for replaying. The answer for the last question
posed is … Since the last value of b that was set was b = 4, the
last calculation is for a=1, b=4. and c=4.

[PAGE_21]
MATLAB Basics: Scalars 3–15
Code to implement an answer for this question is below. Note the
alternate expression for yVertex. If an instructor feels her
students should sharpen their algebra skills, then requiring the
substitution and manipulation to produce the alternate yVertex
would be a useful extension to the problem spec.
% PROBLEM 3-B.1
clc, clear
a = 1 % LINE 1
b = 3 % LINE 2
c = 2 % LINE 3
xVertex = -b/(2*a) % LINE 4
yVertex = a*xVertex^2 + b*xVertex + c % LINE 5
% substituted expression: yVertex = (-b^2/(4*a)) + c
% bring LINE 2 back from History window, change b = 4,
% execute
% EXECUTE LINE 4 from Hist Win
% EXECUTE LINE 5 from Hist Win
% repeat for other settings of a, b, or c
Problem 3-B.2
The Newtonian gravitational force between two objects (like planets) in space
is calculated using the following formula:
m m 
1 2
F = –G--------------
gravitational 2
 d 
Where G is the universal gravitational constant (6.67210 –11 Nm 2 kg –2), m
1
and m are the masses of the planets in kilograms, d is the distance between
2
planets in meters, and the resultant force is in Newtons.
Using MATLAB, calculate the gravitational forces in the following examples:
Part A The Earth (M = 5.9710 24 kg) and its moon
E
(m = 7.3610 22 kg), assuming an average distance of
M
3.8440310 8 meters separating the Earth and its moon.

[PAGE_22]
3–16 MATLAB Basics: Scalars
Part B The Earth and Sun (m = 1.9910 30 kg), assuming an
S
average distance of 1.49597910 11 meters between the Earth
and the Sun.
Part C An Airbus A320 (74 tons) on the ground and the Earth,
assuming the radius of the Earth is 6,380 km.
Part D An Airbus A320 at 40,000 feet and the Earth, assuming the
radius of the Earth is 6,380 km.
Part E An Airbus A320 at 10,000 miles and the Earth, assuming the
radius of the Earth is 6,380 km. (Assume the A320 was towed
to that location.)
Parts A and B of this problem are relatively simple requiring
application of what the “standard” use of command and history
windows.
Parts C, D, and E however require as well an attention to
physical units and an ability to convert units as needed.
Students can usefully be given this problem with no heads-up
about units. A good web site for obtaining conversion factors
(as of this writing) is …
http://www.chemie.fu-berlin.de/chemistry/general/units_en.html
Students can find conversion factors there need in parts of this
exercise:
1 ton = 907.1847 kg
1 foot = 0.3048 m
1 mi = 1609.344 m
Code meeting the requirements of this problem is below.
% PROBLEM 3-B.2
clc, clear
G = 6.672 * 10^-11 % N m^2 kg^-2
% Part A
m1 = 5.97 * 10^24 % kg, earth
m2 = 7.36 * 10^22 % kg, moon
dist = 3.84403 * 10^8 % m, earth-moon
F_partA = -G * m1 * m2 / dist^2 % N, earth-moon
% Part B
m1 = 5.97 * 10^24 % kg, earth
m2 = 1.99 * 10^30 % kg, sun

[PAGE_23]
MATLAB Basics: Scalars 3–17
dist = 1.495979 * 10^11 % m, earth-sun
F_partB = -G * m1 * m2 / dist^2 % N, earth-sun
% Part C
m1 = 5.97 * 10^24 % kg, earth
m2 = 74 % tons, A320
m2 = m2 * 907.1847 % kg, A320
dist = 6380 % km, earth-A320 ground
dist = dist * 10^3 % m, earth-A320 ground
F_partC = -G * m1 * m2 / dist^2 % N, earth-A320 ground
% Part D
m1 = 5.97 * 10^24 % kg, earth
m2 = 74 * 907.1847 % kg, A320
dist1 = 6380 % km, earth-ground
dist1 = dist1 * 10^3 % m, earth-ground
dist2 = 4*10^4 % ft, ground-A320 flight
dist2 = dist2 * 0.3048 % m, ground-A320 flight
dist = dist1 + dist2 % m, earth-A320 flight
F_partD = -G * m1 * m2 / dist^2 % N, earth-A320 ground
% Part E
m1 = 5.97 * 10^24 % kg, earth
m2 = 74 * 907.1847 % kg, A320
dist1 = 6380 % km, earth-ground
dist1 = dist1 * 10^3 % m, earth-ground
dist2 = 10^4 % mi, ground-A320 flight
dist2 = dist2 * 1609.344 % m, ground-A320 flight
dist = dist1 + dist2 % m, earth-A320 flight
F_partE = -G * m1 * m2 / dist^2 % N, earth-A320 ground
Problem 3-B.3
Money saved in a bank account will compound with the interest according to
the following formula:
 interestRate
newBalance = oldBalance 1+--------------------------------
 100 
The interest rate is given in percentage.
In the MATLAB Command Window, set oldBalance to 1,000 and
interestRate to 6 percent. Calculate newBalance. This is the account
balance after one year.

[PAGE_24]
3–18 MATLAB Basics: Scalars
Store the value of newBalance in the variable oldBalance and drag the
command line that calculates newBalance to the Command Window. Store
the value of newBalance in oldBalance again. Describe what is now
stored in oldBalance.
Use your Command History to calculate the balance after eight years. Find the
answer in no more than two additional steps.
This problem should be easy conceptual for students except for
the last “hook” – Find the answer in no more than two additional
steps. The key to meeting that requirement is to know that mul-
tiple lines in the history window can be selected at once for
execution or for dragging to the command window. After students
attempt this problem for a time, a hint that multiple lines can
be selected would be in order. (The method is to hold down
<ctrl> when clicking another history window line.)
Code follows, then an indication explicitly of how the “two
additional steps” requirement is met. Note that in the code, we
start using semicolons with this problem to suppress command
window echo when we do not need output.
% PROBLEM 3-B.3
clc, clear
oldBalance = 1000; % dollars
interestRate = 6; % percent
newBalance = oldBalance * (1 + .01*interestRate)
oldBalance = newBalance;
% Student executes the 5 lines above to get the value
% after 1 year of interest is earned.
% Then, the 4th line to get the value after 2 years, and
% the 5th line to get the calculation ready for the next
% pass through.
% Continue doing this until the account balance for the
% 8th year is computed. Select multiple lines in the
% history window for execution to find the value for 8
% years.
Executing the statements above will produce the following in the
history window.

[PAGE_25]
MATLAB Basics: Scalars 3–19
Selecting and executing the last two lines from the history
window will produce the balance for the second year, and change
the history window to the following:
Now we are to the point in the problem where we want to compute
the value of the bank balance after eight years, and in only two
steps.

[PAGE_26]
3–20 MATLAB Basics: Scalars
Step 1: select the last four lines in the history window and
execute them. This will give the balance after 4 years in the
command window, and change the history window to the following:
Step 2: select the last eight lines in the history window and
execute. This will produce the balance after eight years in the
command window, as desired.
Problem 3-B.4
Redo Problem 3-B.3. To define balance, use this equation:
 interestRate
balance = balance 1+--------------------------------
 100 
Set balance to 1,000 and interestRate to 6 percent. Using these two
variables and your Command History, calculate the account balance after
eight years.
This problem is a redo of the preceding one with a different
expression to compute balance. There is one difference - instead

[PAGE_27]
MATLAB Basics: Scalars 3–21
of two commands to compute a new balance and reset the old bal-
ance to be the new (as in Problem 3-B.3), here the same effect
is obtained by using one command.
% PROBLEM 3-B.4
clc, clear
balance = 1000; % dollars
interestRate = 6; % percent
balance = balance * (1 + (interestRate/100))
Problem 3-B.5
Put values of three and seven in variables (containers) x and y, respectively.
After assigning the values, switch the values of x and y without using any
numbers. Be sure your code for the swap works by checking the results: check
the values of x and y after your swap code is executed..
Hint: You can use an unlimited number of containers in MATLAB.
One of the reasons for using the container model of variables is
that the “visual thinking” that accompanies an application usu-
ally helps students to understand problems like this one. This
simple sounding problem is not simple for those who have not
programmed before. The reason largely lies in a lack of under-
standing for what an assignment statement accomplishes.
Code follows:
% PROBLEM 3-B.3
clc, clear
x = 3
y = 7
temp = x
x = y
y = temp
Problem 3-B.6
The table below shows the prediction of school administration for the percent
of students who will be graduating and the number of new admissions for

[PAGE_28]
3–22 MATLAB Basics: Scalars
each semester. The enrollment just prior to Fall, 2004 is 7,300. What is the
expected enrollment in Fall 2006? (Do not worry about fractions of students
in your result.)
You should make use of the History Window when you are doing the same
calculation multiple times.
Number of Predicted
New Admissions Graduation (%)
Fall 2004 1,200 11
Spring 2005 740 19
Summer 2005 145 6
Fall 2005 1,450 12
Spring 2006 810 21
Summer 2006 120 4
This problem has an intentional vaguness in it. The issue is
whether the graduation numbers should be derived from the size
of the class body excluding new admits, or if the graduation
numbers should be derived from the entire size of the class
body. There are logical arguments for both positions, but the
real point is that the implementor of a solution does not know
what the writter of the problem spec meant. This situation sets
up the possiblity of a discussion about clarrifying problem
specs if the instructor so desires.
The code below assumes the graduation rate is over all the
students (including new admits).
% PROBLEM 3-B.6
clc, clear
enroll = 7300
numberNew = 1200; gradPercent = 11;
enroll = (enroll + numberNew) * (1 - (gradPercent/100))
% This is all the code that is necessary. Executing this
% code will find the beginning enrollment for Spring 2005.
% Then drag the line from the history window that sets
% numberNew and gradPercent, and reset these variables.
% Finally in the history window execute the line setting

MATLAB Basics: Scalars 3–23
% the enrollment. Repeat for all six semesters in the table.

You should do all the tests suggested in the following problems on the Command

progress in MATLAB. You will learn how to write user-defined functions for

Set x1=7, x2=16, and x3=21. Find the arithmetic and geometric means of
these three numbers. Use MATLAB to test if their arithmetic mean is greater
than their geometric mean.
% PROBLEM 3-B.8
clc, clear
x1 = 7
x2 = 16
x3 = 21
aMean = (x1 + x2 + x3) / 3
gMean = (x1 * x2 * x3)^(1/3)
aMean_greater_gMean = aMean > gMean

Problem 3-B.9
The roots of a quadratic polynomial, 0 = ax 2 +bx+c, can be found with this
equation:
2
–b b –4ac
x = ---------------------------------------
2a
Use MATLAB to determine if both roots of the quadratic polynomial
2
0 = x –21x+98 are greater than ten.
An extension of this problem would be to give several sets of
(a, b, c) and have students use the history window to resolve
for the new values of the coefficients. Note of course that if
(b^2 - 4ac) < 0, then the roots will involve complex numbers.
% PROBLEM 3-B.9
clc, clear
a = 1
b = -21
c = 98
x1 = (-b + sqrt(b^2 - 4*a*c)) / (2*a)
x2 = (-b - sqrt(b^2 - 4*a*c)) / (2*a)
bothRootGreater10 = (x1 > 10) & (x2 > 10)

Problem 3-B.10
The unit circle is a useful tool in under-
standing trigonometric relationships
for right triangles. A unit circle is a cir-
cle with a radius of one.
1
For every right triangle whose hypote- b
nuse is 1, the adjacent and opposite 
side lengths are found with these for- a
mulae:
a = cos
b = sin
Applying the Pythagorean theorem, we find these equations:
2 2
a +b = 1
2 2
cos +sin = 1
Use MATLAB to verify this equality for  = 0, 30, 60, and 90 degrees.
The built-in trigonometric functions in MATLAB always take their arguments
in radians. You will need to convert the given angles to radians.
% PROBLEM 3-B.10
clc, clear
alpha = 0 % degrees
alpha_rad = alpha * (pi/180); % radians
factors = (sin(alpha_rad))^2 + (cos(alpha_rad))^2;
identityWorks = factors == 1
% After executing all commands once, then select and move
% all of them back to the command window from the history
% window. Change the value of alpha. Press return to
% reexecute the entire set of commands.

Problem 3-B.11
Jim argues that he is taller than Carla and Cindy. He is willing to put his
money where his mouth is, but it turns out that Jim is taller than Carla or
Cindy but not both. Jim, Carla, and Cindy are 5.9, 6.1, and 5.7 feet tall,
respectively. Use MATLAB to verify the following:
Part A Jim is not taller than Carla and Cindy.
Part B Jim is taller than Carla or Cindy.
Part C Jim is taller than Carla or Cindy but not both.
Part C can be solved using the solutions from Part A and Part B.
The problem spec does not demand a solution for Part C that uses
solutions to Part A and Part B, but instructors can point out
the possibility after students do this exercise.
% PROBLEM 3-B.11
clc, clear
jim = 5.9
carla = 6.1
cindy = 5.7
% Part A
partA_result = ~( (jim > carla) & (jim > cindy) )
% Part B
partB_result = (jim > carla) | (jim > cindy)
% Part C
partC_result = ( (jim > carla) | (jim > cindy) ) & ...
~( (jim > carla) & (jim > cindy) )
% alternate
% partC_result = partB_result & partA_result
Problem 3-B.12
Suppose every morning you look out the window and make a decision for
how to get to work according to the following rules:

[PAGE_34]
3–28 MATLAB Basics: Scalars
If it is rainy and the traffic is light, OR if you have fewer than 15 min-
utes to get to work, you take your car.
If it is rainy and the traffic is heavy, you take the train.
If it is rainy or snowy, you take your umbrella with you.
If it is not rainy and you have more than 15 minutes, you walk.
If it is snowy and the traffic is heavy, you call in sick.
This morning you look outside and see that it is not rainy (rainyDay=0) but
snowy (snowyDay=1), and the traffic is heavy (heavyTraffic=1). You
have 23 minutes to get to work.
Apply each rule using relational and logical operators of MATLAB and decide
if you will take your car (drive), take the train (catchTrain), walk
(walk), or call in sick (callAndCough), and if you will need to take your
umbrella (takeUmbrella).
If the result you get does not make logical sense to you, examine the rules
again and improve them. Run your example again and see if you get a more
sensible result.
Converting the rules in the problem specification into MATLAB
code will not be difficult for most students once they get the
hang of it.
More difficult will be the consideration of the “goodness” of
the result, and reflection on how to “fix” any logical problems.
A context of “just common sense” will aid students in the task.
Code that implements the original problem spec follows:
% PROBLEM 3-B.12
clc, clear
rainyDay = 0;
snowyDay = 1;
heavyTraffic = 1;
timeAvail = 23; % minutes
drive = (rainyDay & ~heavyTraffic) | ...
(timeAvail < 15)
catchTrain = rainyDay & heavyTraffic
walk = ~rainyDay & timeAvail > 15
takeUmbrella = rainyDay | snowyDay
callAndCough = snowyDay & heavyTraffic

[PAGE_35]
MATLAB Basics: Scalars 3–29
When run, this code will produce the following results:
drive = 0
catchTrain = 0
walk = 1
takeUmbrella = 1
callAndCough = 1
The problem is that we are being told to both walk (i.e., go to
work) and to stay home. Both results should not be indicated.
The problem lies with the third rule that tests if we should
walk. The factor left out is that we should not walk if it is
snowy. Putting this extra part (don’t walk if its snowy) into
the rule give us …
walk = ~rainyDay & ~snowyDay & timeAvail > 15
The results of running the code after this change is made are as
follows:
drive = 0
catchTrain = 0
walk = 0
takeUmbrella = 1
callAndCough = 1
This is better, but there is still a problem: we are instructed
to take our umbrella when we are not going to work. Fixing this
problem would be easiest by reversing the order of the last two
code lines, and making takeUmbrella depend on whether or not we
are calling in sick.
% PROBLEM 3-B.12 b... augmented version
clc, clear
rainyDay = 0;
snowyDay = 1;
heavyTraffic = 1;
timeAvail = 23; % minutes
drive = (rainyDay & ~heavyTraffic) | ...
(timeAvail < 15)
catchTrain = rainyDay & heavyTraffic
walk = ~rainyDay & ~snowyDay & timeAvail > 15
callAndCough = snowyDay & heavyTraffic
takeUmbrella = (rainyDay | snowyDay) & ~callAndCough

[PAGE_36]
3–30 MATLAB Basics: Scalars
Problem 3-B.13
Suppose you are working in your company’s accounting department. Your
boss gives you a set of rules and asks you to calculate the salary raise for each
employee. Here are the rules:
If an employee has fewer than six years of experience and has evalua-
tions that are positive OR has six or more years of experience and the
evaluations are not positive, the employee will get an eight percent
raise.
If an employee has six or more years of experience and has evalua-
tions that are positive, the employee will get a 12 percent raise.
Otherwise, the employee will not get any raise.
Write two rules in MATLAB and test your rules with the data in the following
table. Set salary=35000, and apply the MATLAB expressions that imple-
ment the company rules.
Employee Name Years of Experience Evaluations Raise (%)
Ms. Moore 5 1 ?
Ms. Tenenbaum 11 0 ?
Ms. Costanza 3 0 ?
Mr. Cane 9 1 ?
Now condense your two rules into one that will return the desired percent
raise. Test your new rule on the data table again.
This is the most challenging problem in this set. If students
struggle with this problem, then the following “hint” should
help: What is the value of x = 8 * (2 < 4)? This is making use
of MATLAB representation of TRUE and FALSE as 1 and 0.
Answers for the four cases are:
Ms. Moore, 8%

[PAGE_37]
MATLAB Basics: Scalars 3–31
Ms. Tenenbaum, 8%
Ms. Costanza, 0%
Mr. Cane, 12%
Code meeting the original problem spec is as follows:
% PROBLEM 3-B.13
clc, clear
salary = 35000
yrExp = 5, eval = 1
percentRaise_8 = ...
(((yrExp < 6) & eval) | ((yrExp >= 6) & ~ eval))
percentRaise_12 = ((yrExp >= 6)& eval)
raise = 0.08 * percentRaise_8 + 0.12*percentRaise_12
newSalary = salary * (1 + raise)
% Now use the history window to execute the rest of the
% table values for yrExp and eval.
Code meeting the augmented problem spec – to fuse the two rules
of the original spec into one rule – follows:
% ---------------------------------------------------------
% PROBLEM 3-B.13 augmented
clc, clear
salary = 35000
yrExp = 5, eval = 1
raise = 0.08 * (((yrExp < 6) & eval) | ...
((yrExp >= 6) & ~ eval)) + ...
0.12 * ((yrExp >= 6)& eval)
newSalary = salary * (1 + raise)

3–32 MATLAB Basics: Scalars

OLUTIONS FOR HAPTER

AVING OUR ORK IN
This problem is very important. WIthout the ability to store MATLAB work, it
will be difficulty for freshman learners to leverage MATLAB. In the larger view,
the introduction to computational functions in this chapter lays the ground work
for much of what will come in engineering, spanning such disparate topics as
modularity in mechanical design and the use of basic operations in chemical engi-
neering. In a very real sense, the ability to effectively decompose and modularized
conceptual thinking about man made artifact is a central facet of most engineering
disciplines.
Set A: Nuts and Bolts Problems for Saving Your Work
Problem 4-A.1
By default, which folder does MATLAB save files in? Which folder will
MATLAB search first to load or run saved files? Write your answer in a con-
cise paragraph or two.
The answer in both cases is the current directory (or connected
directory).

[PAGE_40]

The purpose of the Current Director window is to graphically
show the contents of the default directory – the directory where
MATLAB saves files unless told otherwise, and where it looks for
files first.

[PAGE_42]

The values of a, b, and  should not be set in your script file. Rather the
script file will use the values of the variables you set in the Command Win-

units.

% file name => computeSASarea.m
% Computes the area of a triangle knowing two sides and the
% angle between the two sides.
% Assumed variables:
% a, b: two sides of the triangle (ft)
% alpha, the angle between a and b (deg)
% sample set up:
% a = 12; b = 2; % feet
% alpha = 78; % deg
% name of computed variables
% SAS_TriangleArea
alphaRad = alpha * (pi/180); % deg
SAS_TriangleArea = (1/2) * a * b * sin(alphaRad) % sq ft

This exercise has several points that should be emphasized.
First, note that a “call” to a script file from another script
is simply the name of the script. The script file name must not
be used with an assignment statement to set a variable – as we
will shortly do with function calls. Students most likely to
trip on this point are students who have prior programming expe-
rience.
Secondly, if students take this simple sounding problem
seriously, they will discover (probably) that using scripts to
call other scripts can get tedious because the names of internal
variables must be known. This point is made in the text of this
chapter, but from a freshman learner’s viewpoint, it is somewhat
hard to grasp.
Thirdly, at instructor option, this exercise can be used to help
students start thinking about how to test MATLAB code. The
comments at the end of the script below suggest setting up a
square and using it for test.
Finally, a variation on this exercise is to give the word
description given in the comments of the code below as the
problem spec, and require the students to make a sketch like
that shown in the actual problem spec.
% file name => twoTriangles.m
% Computes the area of a rhombus constructed of two
% triangles: T1 and T2. For T1 we know two sides a and b,
% and the angle alpha which a makes with b. For T2, the side
% b is common with T1, and we know the other two sides c and
% d. Areas of T1 and T2 are found using other scripts, then
% the area of the rhombus is found by adding T1 and T2.
% Assumed variables:
% a, b, c, d as described above (ft)
% alpha as described above (deg)
% sample set up:
% a=3; b=5; c=7; d=15; % feet

[PAGE_45]
Saving Your Work in MATLAB 4–39
% alpha=32; % deg
% name of computed variables
% sumBothTriangles
% USES SCRIPTS
% computeSASarea and computeSSSarea
% Use computeSAS area to compute area of triangle formed
% by a-ALPHA-b. Note the variables expected by computeSAS
% area are compatible with the varaibles expected by
% twoTriangles.
computeSASarea
% Use computeSSSarea to compute area of triange formed by
% b-c-d. Note that computeSSSarea expects the three sides
% to be named a-b-c. Thus a, b, and c will have to be
% reset before calling computeSSSarea.
a = b;
b = d;
c = d;
computeSSSarea
% Now just add the results.
sumBothTriangles = SAS_TriangleArea + SSS_TriangleArea

% a=10; b=10*sqrt(2); c=10; d=10; % feet
% alpha=45; % deg

This exercise is similar to the A.7, but adds yet more complex-

using scripts: the need to know the internal names of variables.

b = eTOP;

% total area is just the sum of the three parts computed

sumAreaParallelogram = area_Alpha + area_ThreeSides + ...

function angRad = deg2rad(angDeg)
% converts an angle from degrees to radians
% INPUT: angDeg, scalar, an angle in degrees
% OUTPUT: angRad, scalar, equivalent angle in radians
% sample call:
% angleRad = deg2rad(45)
angRad = (pi/180) * angDeg; % radians

Problem 4-A.17
Which type of error is easier to detect a syntax or a runtime error? Why?
Write your answer in one or two concise paragraphs.
A syntax error is typically much easier to detect than a runtime
error. Syntax errors are trapped by MATLAB because a syntax
error is caused by a misuse in some way of the language of
MATLAB. A runtime (or logic) error on the other hand involves no
misuse of the MATLAB language, but rather a flaw in the logic of
the code.
Problem 4-A.18
Identify and correct the syntax errors in the following commands. After you
believe you see the problem, and the correction you need, then type the origi-
nal error and your correction into the Command Window to see if you are cor-
rect.
Part A y = 5(8-5)^2/7
Part B tan45 = sin[45(pi/180)]/cos[45(pi/180)];
Part C sinSquare45 = sin^2(45*pi/180)
Part D EricAge + 7 = 41
Part E (4.7) =< (10/4)
Part F (5/2) = (10/4)

[PAGE_55]
Saving Your Work in MATLAB 4–49
Part A: forgot multiplication sign after 5…
y = 5*(8-5)^2/7
Part B: multiple errors: (1) sine and cosine take round
parenthesis, not square brackets, (2) forgot
multiplication sign in both sine and cosine terms…
tan45 = sin(45*(pi/180))/cos(45*(pi/180));
Part C: raising a sine factor is accomplished by putting the
<caret> *after* the entire sine term …
sinSquare45 = sin(45*pi/180)^2
Pard D: the left hand side of a assignment statement can
include a variable name or variable names only. No
operators or functions are allowed on the left hand
side. It is difficult to “guess” at what the author of
the code line in Part D meant. When faced with this
kind of situation, a reasonable response is to simply
ask the author what operation was intended.
Part E: wrong order for the LESS THAN OR EQUAL TO symbols.
(4.7) <= (10/4)
Part F: the equality relational operator is a double equal
symbol.
(5/2) = = (10/4)

The total interest on an initial investment of M dollars compounded annually at
r% interest is given by …
k
 r 
interestEarned = M 1+ --------- –1
 100
Use the following function to calculate the total interest and overall balance:
function [interest,balance]=bankAccount(M, r, k)
% This function calculates the account balance
% compounded annually over the interest rate,
% after a specified number of years
% Inputs: Initial investment: M (dollars)
% percent interest rate: r (percent)

[PAGE_56]
4–50 Saving Your Work in MATLAB
% number of years: k (yrs)
% Outputs: interest earned: interest (dollars)
% bank account balance: balance (dollars)
interest = M((1+(r/100))^k-1);
balance = M+interest*k;
Create a copy of the function above using the MATLAB editor and save it to
your local machine.
Find and fix the syntax error(s).
Detect the runtime error(s) and fix them.
There is one syntax error: the command that computes “interest”
is missing a multiplication sign.
There are two runtime errors. First the “r” variable stands for
the interest rate as a percent, but the command computing the
“interest” treats “r” as a decimal.
Second, the command for “balance” is conceptually wrong.
The two lines of operating code in the function should be …
interest = M*((1+r)^k-1);
balance = M+interest;
Problem 4-A.20
The area of a triangle for which all side lengths are known can be found using
Heron’s formula …
triangleArea = SS –aS–bS–c
S is called the semi-perimeter and has the following value:
a+b+ c
S = ---------------------
2
A novice programmer writes the following function to implement Heron’s for-
mula:

[PAGE_57]
Saving Your Work in MATLAB 4–51
function area = SSSTriArea(a,b,c)
% This function calculates the area of a triangle
% for which the side lengths are known.
% Inputs: Side lengths of triangle: a (ft),
% b(ft), c(ft)
% Output: Area of triangle: area (sq ft)
A = sqrt(S)*(S-a)*(S-b)*(S-a);
S = a+b+c;
Create this function and save to your hard disk.
Find and fix the syntax error(s).
Using the MATLAB debugging tool, find the runtime error(s), and fix them.
Try your function on a right triangle with side lengths 3, 4, and 5 feet using the
following command line:
>> triArea = SSSTriArea(3,4,5)
Calculate this triangle’s area by hand as well to test your answer.
The syntax error is that S is not computed before it is used.
The order of the line computing A and S should be reversed.
One runtime error is that the command computing S is missing a
divisor of 2.
A second runtime error is in the command computing A: the SQRT
should run across the right hand side of the command, not be
applied only to S.
A third runtime error is that the output variable “area” is not
set in the function.
A fourth runtime error is that the term in the calculation of
the area (S-a) is duplicated. One of the (S-a) terms should be
changed to (S-c).
The two lines from “SSSTriArea” should read as follows:
S = (a+b+c) / 2;
area = sqrt(S*(S-a)*(S-b)*(S-c));

Problem 4-B.1
You are designing a storage silo for corn. Your design uses a right prism with
equilateral triangular bases. In your first try, each side of the triangular end
plates has a length of 30 meters and the height of the silo is 10 meters. This
silo design holds enough corn to meet requirements.
Make a rough sketch of the first silo design. (If you are unsure what a right
prism is, look it up using Google.)
On further consideration, you see that the barn that houses the silo is short on
floor space. Suppose that to make the base of the silo have a smaller footprint,
you redesign a taller and skinnier silo but with the constraint that it must hold
the same amount of corn as the first design. You decide that the redesigned
silo should have a footprint only 75 percent of the first design but hold an
equal amount of corn.
Make a rough sketch of the second silo design.
Your problem: Determine the dimensions of the second (and final) version of
the silo. (Your solution will include the length of a side of triangular end caps
and the height of the prism.)
To solve this problem, write the following script files:
- eqTriangleArea, whose purpose is to calculate the area of an
equilateral triangle given the length of a side (in meters)
- eqTriangleSide, whose purpose is to compute the side of an
equalaterial triangle given its area (in square meters)
- siloProblem, whose purpose is to compute the solution to this
problem using the two other script files (This is the top-level script
file.)
The comments in a script file must include the variable’s names that
must be set before the script is run and the names of variables set by
action of the script. If you don’t know the mathematical formula you
need to solve this problem, look up equilateral triangle using Google.
Be sure to test your implementation.

A major point of this
exercise is to drive home the point that making even simple
programs with scripts can be quite tedious because of the need
to keep variable names internal to each script in mind while
writing other scripts. There are only three scripts in the
problem spec of this problem. Yet even at this scale, students
are likely to have some difficulty.
Notice the way that variables which must be set prior to the
main script running are echoed to the command window at the
beginning of the main script, and how the variables there are to
be computed are also echoed to the command window at the end of
the main script. Although this is useful, it is important for
students to understand that the central action of scripts is to
set variables in the workspace.
Scripts that implement the problem spec of this exercise
follows.

% problem spec.

% eqTriangleAreaFunc, eqTriangleSideFunc

volume_init = area_init * height_init; % cu m

Problem 4-B.3
The sketch at

Tugboat A’s
direction of pull, 35 degrees from the horizontal in the specific case shown.
Finally, the sketch shows Tugboat B pulling the barge at an unknown angle, X
degrees, in the negative direction from the horizontal.
The goal of the two tugboats is to pull the barge straight down the river, which
means along the horizontal. Part of the force exerted by each is along the hor-
izontal while part of the force exerted by each is along an axis that is 90
degrees rotated from the horizontal. In order for the combined pull of the two
tugboats to move the barge straight down the river, the pull of Tugboat A in
the vertical direction must be as large as the pull of Tugboat B in the vertical
but opposite in direction.
Write a Matlab function that calculates the angle labeled X in the sketch.
Your function should take three inputs: the force exerted by Tugboat A (in
lbs), the force exerted by Tugboat B (in lbs), and the angle Tugboat A’s pull
makes with the horizontal (in degrees). Your function should compute and
output the angle that Tugboat B’s pull makes with the horizontal (in degrees)
to satisfy the goal of moving the boat straight down the river. In addition to
the standard comments, include in your function any comments about
assumptions you must make to solve this problem. Call your function
angle_TwoTugboats.
Test your function by executing the following:

[PAGE_64]
4–58 Saving Your Work in MATLAB
>> angB = angle_TwoTugboats(2000, 4000, 35)
For most freshman learners, understanding this problem will be
dependent on understanding the diagram given in the problem
spec.
The code below is an implementation for one solution.
function tugB_angle = ...
angle_TwoTugboats(tugA_force, tugB_force, tugA_angle)
% Compute the angle of Tugboat B in the diagram of the
% problem spec.
% INPUT: tugA_force, scalar, force A exerts (lbs)
% tugB_force, scalar, force B exerts (lbs)
% tugA_angle, scalar, angle A makes to horizontal
% (deg)
% OUTPUT: tugB_angle, scalar, angle B makes to horizontal
% (deg)
% sample call:
% angB = angle_TwoTugboats(2000, 4000, 35)
% The problem spec set the only requirement to be that the
% barge must go “straight” down the river - i.e., along the
% horizontal. This means that the component of the force of
% TugA along the “vertical” the must be equal but opposite to
% TugB along the “vertical.” (Vertical would be 90 degrees
% to the horizontal line marked in the problem spec.)
tugA_rads = tugA_angle * (pi/180); % radians
tugA_vertical = tugA_force * sin(tugA_rads); % lbs
sin_tugB_rads = tugA_vertical / tugB_force; % lbs
tugB_rads = asin(sin_tugB_rads); % radians
tugB_angle = tugB_rads * (180/pi); % deg
Problem 4-B.4
The transient behavior of RLC circuits (containing resistors, inductors, and
capacitors) plays an important role in the design of electronic components.
Suppose an open RLC circuit has an initial charge of q (in coulombs) stored
0
within a capacitor. When the circuit is closed the charge flows out of the
capacitor, leaving a charge q (in coulombs) after a time t given by the follow-
ing relation:

[PAGE_65]
Saving Your Work in MATLAB 4–59
R
– ------ t
2L  1  R 2 0.5 
q = q e cos ------- – ------ t
0  LC 2L 
R is the resistor in the circuit, L is the inductor in the circuit, C is the capacitor
in the circuit, q is the initial charge of the capacitor in the circuit, t is time
0
since the circuit has been closed, and q is the charge remaining on the capaci-
tor at time t. The standard units for R, L, C, q (and q ), and t are ohms, hen-
0

Write a MATLAB function that takes five inputs for an RLC circuit: R (in
ohms), L (in henries), C (in farads), q (in coulombs), and t (in seconds).
0
Your function should compute and output one variable: q, the charge remain-
ing in the capacitor at time t.
Name your function q_RLC.
Perform an initial test of your function by executing the following:
>> R = 100; % ohms
>> L = 9.2; % henries
>> C = 0.0025; % farads
>> q0 = 1000; % columbs
>> chargeRemaining_00 = q_RLC(R,L,C,q0,0)
Your answer should be the value of q at time equal to zero, i.e., 1,000 cou-

Now run your function q_RLC for t=0.1, t=0.2, t=0.3, and t=0.4

Create a plot of Charge versus Time for the five value pairs you have.
How would you characterize the relation between charge remaining and time?
Write a concise paragraph describing your characterization.
This exercise is aimed at providing freshman learners experience
in writing a relatively complex MATLAB expression. The key les-
son that should be learned is to decompose a complex expression,
write expressions for each of the resulting fragments, then com-
pose.
The function below meets the problem specs. A second function
below will create a plot that is part of the spec, but note that
the plot is specified, not the tool used to create the plot.

[PAGE_66]
4–60 Saving Your Work in MATLAB
Students familiar with EXCEL can use it to create the plot, or
as a fall back, a hand drawn plot will suffice.
The relation between charge remaining and time is an exponential
decay.
function q = q_RLC(R, L, C, q0, t)
% compute the charge in an RLC circuit
% INPUT: R, scalar, the circuit resistance (ohms)
% L, scalar, the circuit inductance (henries)
% C, scalar, the circuit capacitance (farads)
% q0, scalar, the initial circuit charge (coulombs)
% t, scalar, time since the circuit was closed (sec)
% OUTPUT: q, scalar, charge remaining in the circuit
% after t seconds
% sample call:
% qRem = q_RLC(100, 9.2, 0.0025, 1000, 0)
eTerm = exp(-R*t/(2*L));
cosTerm = cos( sqrt((1/(L*C)) - (R/(2*L))^2 ) * t);
q = q0 * eTerm * cosTerm;
function q_RLC_plot
% makes the plot speced in Problem B.4
t = 0:.1:.4;
for i = 1:length(t)
q(i) = q_RLC(100, 9.2, 0.0025, 1000, t(i));
end
figure
plot(t, q, '--rs','LineWidth',2,...
'MarkerEdgeColor','k',...
'MarkerFaceColor','g',...
'MarkerSize',10)
xlabel('time (sec)', 'FontWeight', 'bold');
ylabel('charge remaining (coulombs)', 'FontWeight', 'bold');
grid on
titleStr = ['q = q0 * exp(-R*t/(2*L)) * ', ...
'cos( sqrt((1/(L*C)) - (R/(2*L))^2 ) * t)'];
title(texlabel(titleStr), 'FontSize', 14)

Your job is to calculate the fence cost required for the field. From land
records, you know the value of A. From measurement, you know the value of
S. And you are given the cost per linear meter of the fence.
Work out a solution to this problem on paper. Then implement your solution
by developing MATLAB functions. Do not do this problem by writing one

1. The length of W (meters) given the length of S (meters).

[PAGE_68]
4–62 Saving Your Work in MATLAB
This exercise should focus the student on issues of decomposi-
tion to make problem solving modular – both to make implementa-
tions be reusable and to enable more straightforward solutions.
A solution follows.
function cost = priceFenceTri(fieldArea, isSide, unitCostFence)
% Compute the cost of the fence around a field specified in
% the problem description.
% INPUT: fieldArea, scalar, the total area of field (sq m)
% isSide, scalar, length of an isosceles side (m)
% unitCostFense, scalar, unit cost of fence (%/m)
% OUTPUT: cost, scalar, the total cost of a fence around the
% perimeter of the field ($)
% sample call:
% cost = priceFenceTri(100, 10, 2.25)
% calls:
% area_rtIsTri, wGivenS
areaInEnds = 2 * area_rtIsTri(isSide);
areaInRect = fieldArea - areaInEnds;
lengthRec = length_rec(areaInRect, wGivenS(isSide));
totalPerimeter = 2*lengthRec + 4*isSide;
cost = totalPerimeter * unitCostFence;
function side = length_rec(area, width)
% Compute the side length of a rectangle given its
% area and width.
% INPUT: area, scalar, the area of the rectangle (sq m)
% width, scalar, the width of the rectangle (m)
% OUTPUT: side, scalar, the width of the rectangle (m)
% sample call:
% s = length_rec(100, 20)
side = area / width; % m
function area = area_rtIsTri(isocSide)
% compute area of a right isosceles triangle given the
% length of one of the isosceles sides (in meters)
% INPUT: isocSide, scalar, isosceles side of triangle (m)
% OUTPUT: area, scalar, area of triangle (sq m)
% sample call:
% A = area_rtIsTri(10)
% calls:
% wGivenS
base = wGivenS(isocSide);
area = base * (base/2) / 2; % sq m
function baseSide = wGivenS(isocSide)

[PAGE_69]
Saving Your Work in MATLAB 4–63
% computes the length of the base of a right isosceles
% triangle given the length of an isosceles side
% INPUT: isocSide, scalar, length of an isosceles side (m)
% OUTPUT: baseSide, scalar, length of the base (m)
% sample call:
% base = wGivenS(10)
% Construct a line from d that cuts W in half. This line
% will make a right angle with W, by symmetry. Also by
% symmetry, the angle edc will be bisected, thus it will
% be an
baseSide = sqrt(2) * isocSide;
Problem 4-B.6
Suppose you write the following function to find the square of the ratio of two

function divsq = ratioSquare(x1, x2)
% This function finds the square of the ratio of
% two numbers
% Inputs x1 and x2, simple numbers

The runtime error is in the misuse of the precedence of MATLAB.

function should read

Problem 4-B.7
You write the following function to add three numbers at a time:
function [result] = adding(i1,i2,i3);
% This function returns the sum of its three
% scalar inputs
% Inputs: i1, i2, i3 ... are three real numbers
% Outputs: result is the sum of the three numbers
temp = i1 + i2;
result = temp + i3;
Then you call adding from the Matlab Command Window:
>> a = 2
>> temp = 3
>> c = adding(a, a, a)
When execution of adding completes, what is the value of temp?
Solve the problem by hand first, then write the function and run it to test your
answer.
The simple exercise is to underline for students the concepts of
the workspace, and in particular the workspace of a function.
The value of “temp” after “adding” is executed is 3. The
variable named “temp” inside the function is different from the
variable “temp” defined in the global workspace.

Write a Matlab function called qRootsGreaterTen that inputs coeffi-

1 2

1 2
qRootsGreaterTen, form a logical expression that tests to see if both are
greater than 10 and makes certain that the values are real numbers.)

[PAGE_72]
4–66 Saving Your Work in MATLAB
There is a MATLAB built-in function “imag” that will extract the
imaginary part of a complex number, and thus can be used to test
if a number is real. However, using the hint given in the prob-
lem spec should suffice.
Code follows.
function greater10 = qRootsGreaterTen(a,b,c)
% Computes the two roots of a quadratic, and if both roots
% are real numbers and greater than 10, then return 1, else
% return 0.
% INPUT: a,b,c... scalars, the coefficients in
% f(x) = ax^2 + bx + c
% OUTPUT: greater10, either 0 or 1
% sample call:
% x = qRootsGreaterTen(1, 2, 3)
sqRtTerm = b^2 - 4*a*c;
x1 = (-b + sqrt(sqRtTerm)) / (2*a);
x2 = (-b - sqrt(sqRtTerm)) / (2*a);
greater10 = (sqRtTerm >= 0) & (x1 > 10) & (x2 > 10);
Problem 4-B.10
Automobile transmissions are used to reduce
the torque (the turning force on a shaft) while

or coupled. Real transmissions typically have

This intuition leads to a relation between the angular speed of Gear 1, its
radius, and the angular speed of Gear 2 and its radius. Assuming that the teeth

Write a function that inputs the radius of Gear 1, the angular speed of Gear 1,

function gear2_requiredRad = ...
gear2_rotSpeed(gear1Rad, gear1AngSp, gear2DesAngSp)
% For a two-gear system, computes the output gear radius
% required to produce a given output gear angular speed,
% also given input gear radius and angular speed.
% INPUT: gear1Rad, scalar, input gear radius
% gear1AngSp, scalar, input gear angular speed

% rad2 = gear2_rotSpeed(rad1, angSp1, angSp2)
gear2_requiredRad = gear1Rad * (gear1AngSp/gear2DesAngSp);

function R = resistanceGivenV_I(V, i, e)
% For an voltage and resistance circuit, computes the
% resistance R across a load given the current through the
% load i, the voltage across the load e, and the driving
% voltage V.
% INPUT: V, scalar, voltage driving the circuit (V)
% i, scalar, current through the load (amps)
% e, scalar, voltage across the load (V)

% voltsBat = 30; current = 5; voltsDrop = 8;
% R = resistanceGivenV_I(voltsBat, current, voltsDrop)
R = (V - e) / i;

function D = cylDiameter(vol)

Problem 4-B.13
You are testing the following trig identity:
2 2
sin + cos = 1 for all 
You write a function called pythagorasForUnitCircle and run it for 0,
30, 60, and 90 degrees. Unfortunately, the program produces large numbers of
error messages. You conclude the identity is incorrect. Your conclusion is
obviously wrong, and an error message indicates bad MATLAB syntax.
Study your code below carefully. Determine the syntax and/or runtime error(s)
and fix them.
Run the code with several inputs to test the improved version of
pythagorasForUnitCircle:
function trueOrFalse = pythagorasForUnitCircle(angle)
% This function is written to verify the identity
% cos^2(angle)+sin^2(angle) = 1
% Input: angle... an angle in degrees
% Output: trueOrFalse ... 1 if identity is verified,
% 0 otherwise
sincos = cos^2(angle)+sin^2(angle);
trueOrFalse = (sincos=1);
There are two syntax errors in the function: (1) the test on
equality in the last line should be “==” and (2) the cosine and
sine squared terms in the next to last line should be of form
like cos(x)^2.
There is also one runtime error: the angle is input in degrees
and must be converted to radians. The two lines of code should
read as follow:
angle = angle * (pi/180); % radians
sincos = cos(angle)^2 + sin(angle)^2;

Problem 4-B.14
You need to write a MATLAB function to compute L(x) given by the follow-
ing equation (where 0x1):
0.625 –1.625
 x  1–x
L = 100 ------- -----------
0.6  0.4 
The function you write as a first draft is the following:
function L = computeL(x)
% This function calculates L(x) per the problem spec
% Input: x
% Output: L
L = 100 * x/0.6^0.625 * 1-x/0.4^-1.625;
Find and correct the errors in your first draft.
Although this is the last problem in this chapter, it is
straightforward. The code below corrects the errors, which are
caused by unintended application of precedence rules.
function L = computeL(x)
% This function calculates L(x) per the problem
% spec
% Input: x
% Output: L
% L2 = 100 * x/0.6^0.625 * 1-x/0.4^-1.625;
firstTerm = (x/0.6)^0.625;
secondTerm = ((1-x)/0.4)^-1.625;
L = 100 * firstTerm * secondTerm;
% Or in one line...
% L = 100 * (x/0.6)^0.625 * ((1-x)/0.4)^-1.625;

S C 5
OLUTIONS FOR HAPTER
V O
ECTOR PERATIONS
The problems in this chapter in part are exemplars of what distinguishes MAT-
LAB: a very strong ability to perform multiple operations in one clear line of code
by using vector operations. Students should try to keep in mind as they work these
problems that a good deal of the material in this chapter can be understood as a
generalization of scalar operations.
Students typically have difficulty with content addressing, using the find
built-in function to identify the locations in vectors whose contents satisfy set con-
ditions. It is important especially that they master this technique in order to make
good use of the power offered in MATLAB.
Set A: Nuts and Bolts Problems for Vectors

Problem 5-A.6
Create a row vector a6 starting with the element 1, ending with the element
10, with five equally spaced points in the vector. Use linspace.
a6 = linspace(1,10,5)
Its important that students understand the problem spec “equally
spaced points.” This matches the definition of linspace. If
however, the problem spec had been for “equal length intervals”
then the solution would have been linspace(1,10,6).
Problem 5-A.7
Create a row vector a7 starting with the element 1, ending with the element

numPts = 5
numIntervals = numPts - 1
legnthTotalInt = 10 - 1
lengthBetweenPoints = legnthTotalInt / numIntervals

[PAGE_82]
5–76 Vector Operations
a7 = 1:lengthBetweenPoints:10
The point of this exercise and the one that follows is to show
students that the colon operator fits for some operations, but
not for others. The colon operator is designed for operations
when the length between points and the final point in a vector
under construction are known. The “easy” MATLAB function to use
for this exercise is linspace.

Problem 5-A.12
In MATLAB, define variable x as follows:
>> x = [3, 9, 4, 99, 2]
Set a variable y to be the first and fourth elements of x.
y = x([1,4])
Students many times will miss the square bracket and try to use
x(1,4). The error being made is to not understand that x([1,4])
is really a two step operation: first create an index vector,
then use the index vector to address the desired elements of x.
Problem 5-A.13

[PAGE_84]

operator in your solution.
y = x(1:3)
Note that the colon operator can easily be confused by students
with the colon symbol use to access all elements of a vector (or
array).
Problem 5-A.14
In MATLAB, define variable x as follows:
>> x = [3, 9, 4, 99, 2]
Set a variable y to be the third through the last element of x so your solution
works no matter how many elements are in x.
y = x(3:end)
The use of “end” inside an index is important in MATLAB because
it allows general solutions – the phrase “works no matter how
many elements …” should be a tip off to students in this
exercise to use “end.”

[PAGE_85]

y = x(end-1:end)
A common error students make on this exercise is to write x(end-
1, end) again because of a misunderstanding of indexing.

Problem 5-A.18
In MATLAB, define variable x as follows:
>> x = [3, 9, 4, 99, 2]

the length of x.

Using an index of “end” is relatively straightforward for
students for READ operations. It is far less intuitive at the
outset for WRITE operations.

Problem 5-A.35
In MATLAB, define variables x and y as follows:
>> x = [3; 9; 4; 5; 2]
>> y = [2; 1; 4; 3; 2]
Create a new vector z in which each element is three times the value of the
corresponding value in x, divided by five times the value of the corresponding
value in y.
z = 3*x ./ (5*y)
The multiplication operations need not use the DOT because one
side is a scalar. When introduced to cell-by-cell operations,
students have a tendency to use a DOT for every multiplication,
division, and exponentiation. It will be helpful later if
students now use the DOT versions as needed. That is, if

[PAGE_93]
Vector Operations 5–87
students now think through when to use the DOT and when it is
not necessary, they will be in much better shape later.

Problem 5-A.38
In MATLAB, define variables x and y as follows:
>> x = [3; 9; 5; 5; 1]
>> y = [2; 1; 4; 6; 2]

[PAGE_94]
5–88 Vector Operations
Find the indices for all elements in x such that corresponding elements of y
are twice as big.
ndx = find(x == 0.5*y)
Although this is a simple exercise combining a relational, cell-
by-cell comparison and the find operator, this is an important
exercise to be certain students understand. The concept of
“index” is not straightforward for many freshman learners of
MATLAB.
Problem 5-A.39
In MATLAB, define variables x and y as follows:
>> x = [3; 9; 5; 5; 1]
>> y = [2; 1; 4; 6; 2]
Find the indices for elements in x so the corresponding elements of y are
twice as big as the element in x or the corresponding elements of y are less
than x.
ndx = find( (2*x == y) | (y < x) )
The inner parentheses around the relational tests are
technically not needed. However, readability is increased by
including them.
Problem 5-A.40
In MATLAB, define variables x and y as follows:
>> x = [3; 9; 5; 5; 1]
>> y = [2; 1; 4; 6; 2]

[PAGE_95]
Vector Operations 5–89
Find the indices for all elements in x so neither the corresponding elements of
y are twice as big as the element in x nor the corresponding elements of y are
less than x.
ndx = find( ~( (2*x == y) | (y < x ) ) )
A useful additional exercise is to ask students what the
relationship should be between the answer for this exercise and
the previous exercise. And why. Depending on time and the level
of students, a discussion of deMorgan’s rules could also follow
from this exercise.
Problem 5-A.41
In MATLAB, define variables x and y as follows:
>> x = [3; 9; 5; 5; 1]
>> y = [2; 1; 4; 6; 2]
Find the locations and values of all the elements in x that have values between
4 and 10.
ndx = find( (4 < x) & (x < 10) )
values = x(ndx)
This type of question (“find values of X between …”) beginning
students typically answer with a phrase like 4 < x < 10, which
appears to be a more direct translation of the problem spec. To
straighten students out on this point is to emphasize to them
the way MATLAB processes expressions, and in particular the way
that in the absence of an applicable precedence rule MATLAB
processes left to right. The issue also underscores the value of
using additional parentheses to help in “understanding.”

x(ndx) = y(ndx)

allRes = [firstRes,secondRes]; % deg C
lengthsOK = (length(firstRes) + length(secondRes) ) ...
== length(allRes)

Problem 5-B.4
Use two methods to create a vector with 30 elements, where each element is
the number 30. Use built-in functions zeros in one method and ones in the
other method.
oneMethod = 30 + zeros(1,30)
secondMethod = 30 * ones(1,30)
The point of this exercise is for students to realize that there
is more than one way to build a vector. A follow on exercise
would be to ask students to test to be sure the two methods
yield the same result. One such test would be … all(oneMethod ==
secondMethod)

[PAGE_100]
5–94 Vector Operations
Problem 5-B.5
Create the following vectors using the colon operator and the built-in function
linspace methods:
Part A A vector whose elements are all odd numbers between 1 and
191
Part B A vector whose elements are 99 evenly spaced numbers
between 5 and 148
partA_colon = 1:2:191
partA_linspace = linspace(1,191,((191-1)/2)+1)
Students should note: The colon operator is the easier way to
solve Part A.
partB_colon = 4:(148-4)/(99-1):148
partB_linspace = linspace(4,148,99)
Students should note: the linspace operator is the easier way to
solve Part B.
This problem also gives the instructor an opportunity to
emphasize the importance of using containers for accuracy. You
will observe that many freshman learners will be inclined to
calculate the interval size and use the number they see on the
screen in the colon operator. However, this truncated value of
the interval size will result in a vector that ends with
146.5318 instead of 148.
Problem 5-B.6
We would like to display a temperature given in oF on three temperature
scales, oF, oC, and oK, in a vector of length 3. The conversion from oF to oC
and from oC to oK are given as follows:

[PAGE_101]
Vector Operations 5–95
tempFahrenheit–32
tempCelsius = ------------------------------------------------------
1.8
tempKelvin = tempCelsius+273.15
Write a MATLAB function that accepts a scalar for temperature in Fahrenheit
degrees as input and outputs a vector of degrees in oF, oC, and oK.
function outTempsV = convertFahrenheit(degF)
% converts temperature in Fahrenheit to Celsius and Kelvin
% INPUT: temperature in Fahrenheit
% OUTPUT: outTempsV, 3 elecment vector [f, c, k]
% where f is the input Fahrenheit temperature
% c is the equivalent Celsius temperature
% k is the equivalent Kelvin temperature
% sample call:
% allTemps = convertFahrenheit(212)
degC = (degF - 32) / 1.8;
degK = degC + 273.15;
outTempsV = [degF,degC,degK]

Problem 5-B.8
Write a MATLAB function that takes a positive, non-zero integer N and a vec-
tor V as inputs and produces a single output vector such that every Nth ele-
ment in V is set to the value -99.
For example, if N=3, and V=[1 2 3 4 5 6 7], the output returned would
be [1 2 -99 4 5 -99 7].
function V = changeEachNth(N, V)
% modifies a vector input such that every Nth element
% is set it -99, where
% N is also an input
% INPUTS: N a positive integer
% V a numerical vector (row or column)
% OUTPUTS: V a modified version of the input where each Nth
% element is set to -99
V(N:N:end) = -99;

Problem 5-B.10
Use vector x=[4 8 2 1]. Write a MATLAB function that takes no inputs
and outputs a shuffled version of x. The function will shuffle the elements in
vector x by performing the following steps:
1. Switch the first and last elements of x.
2. Switch the first two elements with the last two elements in x.
For the value for x above, the output of your function should be [2 4 1 8].
function V = shuffleInput(V)
% (a) swap first and last elements of V, (b) swap first two
% elements with last two elements
% INPUT: V a vector of at least 4 elements
% OUTPUT: modified version of V per problem spec
temp = V(1);
V(1) = V(end);
V(end) = temp;
temp = V(1:2);
V(1:2) = V(end-1:end);
V(end-1:end) = temp;
Problem 5-B.11
Write a MATLAB function that accepts two integers, a and b, and outputs a
column vector that is composed of every other number between a and b.
function everyOtherV = buildEveryOtherV(a, b)
% creates a vector composed of every other integer between a,b
% INPUTPS: a - integer
% b - integer
% OUTPUT: everyOtherV - column vector of integers starting with
% a, and including every other integer until
% b is reached or exceeded
everyOtherV = (a:2:b)';

Problem 5-B.12
Write a MATLAB function that accepts two scalars, a and b, and an integer c,
and outputs a column vector that is composed of c evenly spaced numbers
between a and b.
function evenlySpacedV = buildVector(a, b, c)
% creates a column vector composed c evenly spaced numbers
% between a and b, with simple use of linspace function
% INPUTPS: a - scalar
% b - scalar
% c - a positive integer
% OUTPUT: evenlySpacedV - column vector of integers starting
% with a, ending with b, and including a
% total of c points
evenlySpacedV = (linspace(a,b,c))'

Problem 5-B.15
When an object of mass m attached to a spring from above is free, two rela-
tions for the two forces are acting on the spring: the gravitational force giving
rise to the weight of the mass (pulling the mass downward) and the force
exerted by the spring (pulling the mass upward). At rest, the two forces must
be equal to each other in magnitude but opposite in sign.
F = –mg
gravitational
F = –k x
spring spring
mg = –k x
spring
The first relation gives the force due to gravity (the weight). The second rela-
tion gives the restoring force due to the spring, where k is the spring con-
spring
stant, i.e., a constant of proportionality that is descriptive for one given spring
and where x is the length that the weight stretches the spring. The third rela-
tion puts it all together since the spring force and the gravitational force are
equal in magnitude but opposite in direction.
We will take the units for the relation mg = -k x to be the following: unit
spring
for mass (m) is kilograms, unit for displacement (x) is meters, unit for the
spring constant (k) is Newtons/meter. The value of g, the acceleration due to
gravity, is 9.81 m/s/s.
The potential energy stored in the string due to this elongation is known to be
(in Newton-meters):
2
kx
potentialEnergy = --------
2
Write a MATLAB function that accepts a vector of extension distances (x)
and a vector of corresponding spring constants (k). Your function should
return a vector of object masses and a vector of potential energies stored in
each spring.

[PAGE_107]
Vector Operations 5–101
Test your function with data for the five spring/mass examples in the figure
below. Your function should work for any number of spring-mass systems.
kk== kk== kk== kk== kk==
990000 NN//mm 440000 NN//mm 880000 NN//mm 11330000 NN//mm 660000 NN//mm
1100 ccmm
1100 ccmm mm
44
1100 ccmm mm
11
1100 ccmm mm
33
1100 ccmm mm
55
mm
22
function [massV, potentialEnergyV] = springsCompute(extDistV,
sprConstV)
% compute suspended mass and potential energy values for
% a set of springs given corresponding static extension
% distances and spring constants
% INPUTS: extDistV, a vector of extension distances (m)
% sprConstV, a vector of spring constants (N/m)
% OUTPUTS: massV, a vector of mass values for each spring (kg)
% potentialEnergyV, a vector of potential energy values
% stored in each spring at the static
% extension position (N-m)
g = -9.81; % accel due to gravity (m/s/s),
% minus sign expressed direction is "down"
massV = -(sprConstV .* extDistV) / g; % (kg)
potentialEnergyV = 0.5 * (sprConstV .* extDistV.^2); % (N-m)
% sample call
% D = [20 50 30 10 40]; S = [9 4 8 3 6] * 10^2;
% [masses, potentialEnergyStored] = springsCompute(D, S)
Problem 5-B.16
Suppose you have a catalogue of automobiles such as in the following table:

(0 - black, 1 - blue, 2 - red)

function selectIDs = chooseCars(idV, colorV, priceV)
% chooses cars from a DB such that the color of the car is black
