Problem 7-B.8
Steel is often produced by blending carbon with other steel alloys that contain
nickel, manganese, and chromium. The percentage of each of these metals in
a blend affects the properties of the resultant steel. For instance, stainless steel
is defined as a ferrous alloy (an alloy containing iron) with a minimum of 10.5
percent chromium content.
Suppose you are working for a steel blending company. Your company pro-
duces steel by combining three different stock alloys that each contain per-
centages of nickel, manganese, chromium, and carbon. The percentages for
each of the three stock alloys your company uses, along with the cost per ton
you have invested in each, are shown in the table below.
Chromium Nickel Manganese Carbon Cost per Ton
(%) (%) (%) (%) ($)
Alloy 1 25 7 5 2 115
Alloy 2 6 5 6 4 92
Alloy 3 11 6 7 5 129
You receive an order for 70 tons of stainless steel that is composed of 40%
Alloy 1, 30% Alloy 2, and 30% Alloy 3. Does this alloy composition produce
stainless steel? What are the overall chromium, nickel, manganese, and car-
bon percentages in the blend you will produce? What is the total cost of this
production?
Instead of doing this problem from scratch each time you get a new order,
your boss has asked you to write a function, Prob7_B_8, that inputs (a) the
total number of tons in an order, (b) a vector containing the percentages of
chromium, nickel, manganese and carbon specified in the order, and (c) a vec-
tor containing the cost you have in each ton for Alloy 1, Alloy 2, and Alloy 3.
Your function should represent internally the percentages of chromium,
nickel, and manganese for all of your stock alloys in an array.
Your function should output the following:
• A vector containing the computed number of tons of each
of your three stock alloys to use to meet the order

[PAGE_220]
7–214 Arrays
• The cost you will have in raw materials to meet the order
• An answer to the question: Does the blend qualify as stain-
less steel? (return a logical value)
Point to think about: Why was it a good idea to utilize the cost of your base
alloys as inputs in your function, while on the other hand the percentage con-
tent makeup of each stock material was represented internally in your func-
tion?
function [alloysTons, overallCost, isStainless] = ...
Prob7_B_8(totOrder,percentAlloys,alloyCosts)
% This function calculates a mixture of all alloys to produce
% steel
% INPUTS: Total order (tons)
% Percent requirement of Alloys in the mixture in a row
% vector
% Alloy costs per ton in a row vector
% OUTPUTS: Tons of Alloy 1, Alloy 2, Alloy 3 in the mixture (a
% row vector)
% Overall cost of the production
% True if the steel is stainless
% Sample line to call the function:
% [alloysTons, overallCost, isStainless] = ...
% prob7_B_8(50,[40 30 30],[100 70 90])
alloyContent = [.25 .07 .05 .02; .06 .05 .06 .04; ...
.11 .06 .07 .05];
percentAlloys = percentAlloys*0.01; % convert percentage
alloysTons = percentAlloys*totOrder;
overallCost = sum(alloysTons.*alloyCosts);
% Finding the ingredients in the alloy mixture:
ingredientPercentages = percentAlloys*alloyContent;
isStainless = ingredientPercentages(1)>=.105;
% Why did we make the costs input but not the alloy contents?
% Because costs might change according to market conditions
% but the alloy content is less prone to change since it
% involves change in the manufacturing process.

Arrays 7–215
Problem 7-B.9
A furniture manufacturer makes dining tables, desks, coffee tables, and end
tables. The material and labor requirement for each type of production is
given in the table below:
Dining Table Desk Coffee Table End Table
Wood (sq ft) 145 130 95 55
Labor (hrs) 12 9 7 5
Selling Price ($) 750 520 340 177
The current cost for wood is $2.25 per sq. ft.; the current cost for labor per
hour is $8.50.
Your supervisor has asked you to write a function, Prob7_B_9, to process
new orders. The orders will come in as quantities of dining tables, desks, cof-
fee tables, and end tables. Your function should deal with these four numbers
as a single input vector and as its first input argument. The second and third
inputs to your function should be the current cost for wood and the current
cost for labor.
Your function should compute and output (a) the total profit that you company
will make on the order, (b) the total amount of wood used in the order, and (c)
the total number of hours required to complete the order.
function [profit,sqFtWood,hrLabor] = ...
Prob7_B_9(productOrders,woodCost,laborCost)
% This function makes a profit and resource analysis
% INPUTS: The order for dining tables, desks, coffee tables,
% and end tables (a row vector)
% Cost of wood (per sq ft)
% Cost of labor (per hour)
% OUTPUTS: Profit made from this order ($)
% Amount of wood used (sq ft)
% Hours of labor spent
% Sample line to call the function:
% [profit,sqFtWood,hrLabor]=prob7_B_9([5 7 3 1],2.25,8.5)
resourceAndPrice = [145 130 95 55;12 9 7 5; 750 520 340 177];
woodLaborPrice = productOrders*resourceAndPrice';
totalCost = [woodCost laborCost]*woodLaborPrice(1:2)';

[PAGE_222]
7–216 Arrays
profit = woodLaborPrice(3)-totalCost;
sqFtWood=woodLaborPrice(1);
hrLabor=woodLaborPrice(2);
Problem 7-B.10
Your friend, Katherine, a stock broker, asks for your help in analyzing stock
prices. She tells you that she keeps daily records for a number of stocks. Her
strategy is to buy stocks whenever the stock price is lower than its average
over the last three days and to sell a given stock if its price is higher than the
three-day average.1
Katherine sends you a sample record of stock prices as shown in the table
below. The first column represents the stock identifier and the remaining col-
umns are stock prices in successive days.
Stock Number Price ($) Price ($) Price ($) Price ($)
April 21st April 22nd April 23rd April 24th
001 14.00 15.25 13.50 15.50
002 21.00 18.50 19.25 19.00
003 7.00 7.75 7.25 7.50
004 32.50 31.25 30.00 30.25
Write a function, Prob7_B_10, that inputs the stock prices in the table as an
array. Your function should compute and output two vectors:
• The identifiers for stocks that should be bought on April
25
• The identifiers for stocks that should be sold on April 25
Each day Katherine wants to play the market with her strategy, she is going to
have to retype the array with current stock prices. This would get old, fast.
What would you like to be able to do to prevent retyping? How could you
implement your idea by extending your function? (For one possible option,
see save and load in MATLAB Help.)
1. The first thing you should tell Katherine is that she is likely to lose her shirt with this strategy!

[PAGE_223]
Arrays 7–217
function [buy, sell] = Prob7_B_10(stockPrices)
% running average analysis for stock exchange
% INPUT: An array of stock identifiers and daily stock prices
% arranged in columns
% OUTPUTS: A column vector of identifiers of stocks to buy
% A column vector of identifiers of stocks to sell
stockIdentifiers = stockPrices(:,1);
averages = mean(stockPrices(:,end-2:end),2);
buyIndices = find(stockPrices(:,end)<averages);
sellIndices = find(stockPrices(:,end)>averages);
buy = stockIdentifiers(buyIndices);
sell = stockIdentifiers(sellIndices);

ROGRAMMING
Problems in this chapter are in general the most difficult in this text, especially for
some of the problems in Set B. This chapter should be the culmination of a num-
ber of threads played out in this textbook, and the problems of Chapter 8 should
help the students bring these threads together.
The most important thread is that students must become independent problem
solvers. Particularly in Set B, there are a number of suggestions made to the
instructor to encourage students to extend beyond the problem spec that is given.
A second thread is that in MATLAB (as is true in any solid computational
environment) there are almost always multiple ways of solving problems. When
using MATLAB, this is particularly true given the availability of loop constructs
and vector operations.
A third thread is that using graphical plotting methods can be helpful for
understanding a situation and that this understanding can open a path to full prob-
lem solution. Some of the problems below have this characteristic.
Finally, a fourth thread is that using MATLAB HELP effectively is essential
for MATLAB users. No university course and no single textbook can possibly
“teach” all the capabilities of MATLAB.

[PAGE_226]
8–220 More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming
Set A: Nuts and Bolts Problems for Programming
Constructs
Problem 8-A.1
Write a MATLAB function, Prob8_A_1, that inputs a a scalar x and returns
a scalar y according to the following rules. Use Form 4 of IF-THEN-ELSE
in your function.
y = –x–  x–
 
y = sinx  for –x
 
y = –x+ x
 
On the surface, this problem is easy. A function that meets the
problem spec is as follows:
function y = Prob8_A_1(x)
% if x<=-pi, outputs -x-pi; if -pi<x<=pi, outputs sin(x);
% if pi<x, outputs -x+pi
% INPUT: x a scalar
% OUTPUT: y a scalar
if x <= -pi
y = -x-pi;
elseif (-pi < x) & (x <= pi)
y = sin(x);
elseif pi < x
y = -x+pi;
else
error('uncovered input')
end
Note that the IF and ELSEIF clauses are used to cover all
“possibilities” that are straightforward to see. In reality, in
this case, all of these “possibilities” taken together are
exhaustive. But it is good practice to write a IF construct to
employ an ELSE clause that will catch unforeseen contingencies -
and produce an error if “hit.”
There are two additional steps that instructors may want to
consider. These two steps are not explicitly written into the
problem. The two provide an example of the type of extension

[PAGE_227]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–221
that instructors might want to make in the problem specs for
this chapter.
First, to emphasize an earlier lesson about solution testing,
you might tell students to test their solution function for this
problem by producing a graph of f(x) = Prob8_A_1(x) for -2< x
< 2A script using a FOR loop to do this testing is as follows:
testX = linspace(-2*pi, 2*pi);
testY = [];
for oneX = testX
testY = [testY, Prob8_A_1(oneX)];
end
figure
plot(testX, testY, 'bo')
grid on
title('Prob8-A-1 results')
xlabel('x')
ylabel('y per problem spec')
Second, a good additional exercise would be to require your
students to build their version of Prob8_A_1 so that it will
work on a vector of input values. A version of Prob8_A_1 meeting
this additional requirement by using a FOR loop is shown below:
function [allX, allY] = Prob8_A_1_modified(allX)
% if x<=-pi, outputs -x-pi; if -pi<x<=pi, outputs sin(x);
% if pi<x, outputs -x+pi
% INPUT: x a vector
% OUTPUT: y a vector of corresponding values
% sample call and test:
% allX = linspace(-2*pi, 2*pi);
% [aX, aY] = Prob8_A_1_modified(allX);
% figure
% plot(aX, aY, 'o'), title('Test Prob 8-A-1 modified')
% grid on
% xlabel('x'), ylabel('y per problem spec')
allY = [];
for x = allX
if x <= -pi
y = -x-pi;
elseif (-pi < x) & (x <= pi)
y = sin(x);
elseif pi < x
y = -x+pi;
else
error('uncovered input')
end
allY = [allY, y];

[PAGE_228]
8–222 More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming
end
Problem 8-A.2
Create a function, Prob8_A_2, that returns the course grade for a given
numerical score for the term. Numerical scores are on a 100-point scale, and
final course grades are on a 4-point scale. Your function must input a numeri-
cal score and return a vector containing the numerical score for a student in
first position and the corresponding course grade in the second position. Here
is the grading scale for the course:
• 90 and above: 4.0
• Between 80 and 89: 3.0
• Between 70 and 79: 2.0
• Between 60 and 69: 1.0
• 59 and below: 0.0
For example, if the input score is 84, your function must return the term grade
3. Again, use Form 4.
This problem is essentially like the preceding problem in form.
The solution below is a vectorized function that goes beyond the
actual problem spec.
The problem spec is intentionally not complete. Note that the
problem spec does not take into account a score of, for example,
89.2. It would be worthwhile to push your students on this point
to reinforce the idea that it is the solver’s responsibility
(i.e., the student’s) to clarify any points found lacking in a
problem specification.
function courseGrade_VECTOR = Prob8_A_2(numericalScore_VECTOR)
% converts a vector of numerical scores to a vector or
% corresponding course grades using the following:
% score < 60 yields 0.0,
% 60 <= score < 70 yields 1.0
% 70 <= score < 80 yields 2.0
% 80 <= score < 90 yields 3.0
% 90 <= score yields 4.0
% INPUTS: numericalScore_VECTOR: vector of term numerical
% scores

[PAGE_229]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–223
% OUTPUTS: courseGrade_VECTOR: vector of corresponding term
% scores
% sample call:
% allNumScores = [55 95 85 65 75 0 100];
% courseGrade_VECTOR = Prob8_A_2(allNumScores)
courseGrade_VECTOR = [];
for one_numScore = numericalScore_VECTOR
if one_numScore < 60
oneCourseGrade = 0.0;
elseif (60 <= one_numScore) & (one_numScore < 70)
oneCourseGrade = 1.0;
elseif (70 <= one_numScore) & (one_numScore < 80)
oneCourseGrade = 2.0;
elseif (80 <= one_numScore) & (one_numScore < 90)
oneCourseGrade = 3.0;
elseif (90 <= one_numScore)
oneCourseGrade = 4.0;
else
error('bad numerical score')
end
courseGrade_VECTOR = [courseGrade_VECTOR, oneCourseGrade];
end
Problem 8-A.3
Write a function, Prob8_A_3, using two scalar inputs x and y and the fol-
lowing code:
if x > 3
if y > x
disp('x is greater than 3 and y is greater than x')
end
end
This code is two nested Form 1 versions of IF-THEN-ELSE.
Modify your function, and save it as Prob8_A_3b, so you have one Form 1
conditional.
Test your revised code with the following pairs of x and y:
x 1 1 4 4 4
y 2 4 3 4 5

[PAGE_230]
8–224 More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming
This question is meant to exercise students’ ability to use log-
ical functions and apply logical functions in IF constructs.
Code meeting the problem spec is below:
function Prob8_A_3b(x,y)
% displays a message if x>3 and y>x
% INPUTS: x, a scalar
% y, a scalar
% OUTPUTS: none
% sample call:
% Prob8_A_3(5,7)
if (x > 3) & (y > x)
disp('x is greater than 3 and y is greater than x')
end
Problem 8-A.4
Are the following Form 1 and Form 2 IF-THEN-ELSE statements function-
ally equivalent? That is, do they generate the same result? Why?
Code 1 Code 2
if x > y
z = 0;
z = 1;
if x > y
else
z = 1;
z = 0;
end
end
Code 1 and Code 2 are functionally equivalent. The point of this
problem is to show students two common alternative forms for
setting a variable dependent on a test.
The topic of which is these is “best practice” is left open by
this problem. In general Code 1 is preferred because it captures
in one code “block” the two options for setting the value of z.

[PAGE_231]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–225
It is left to instructor option whether to include a discussion
of modularity in talking about this problem.
Problem 8-A.5
Write a MATLAB function Form 3 conditional as function, Prob8_A_5, that
inputs an angle in degrees and returns one three-element vector containing the
quadrant of the angle (consider angles between 0 and 360 degrees), and the
sine and cosine of the angle. See the following drawing for quadrants.
QQuuaadd 22 QQuuaadd 11
QQuuaadd 33 QQuuaadd 44
Assume that 0o is in quadrant 1, 90o is in quadrant 2, 180o is in quadrant 3,
and 270o is in quadrant 4.
Use Form 4 of IF-THEN-ELSE for this problem.
A Form 4 IF is requested in this problem specification. However,
specifying a Form 4 IF construct does not pin down how many
clauses there should be in the code. In the code below, again, a
solution is given that allows error trapping.
function [quad, sinAng, cosAng] = Prob8_A_5(angDeg)
% inputs an angle in degrees and ouputs (a) the quadrant in
% which the resides, (b) the sine of the angle, and (c) the
% cosine of the angle
% INPUTS: angDeg, a scalar angle in degrees
% with 0 <= angDeg <= 360
% OUTPUTS: quad, a scalar, the quadrant number of the angle

[PAGE_232]
8–226 More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming
% 0 <= angDeg < 90 => quad 1
% 90 <= angDeg < 180 => quad 2
% 180 <= angDeg < 270 => quad 3
% 0 <= angDeg < 360 => quad 4
% sample call:
% [q, sinA, cosA] = Prob8_A_5(32)
if (0 <= angDeg) & (angDeg < 90)
quad = 1;
elseif (90 <= angDeg) & (angDeg < 180)
quad = 2;
elseif (180 <= angDeg) & (angDeg < 270)
quad = 3;
elseif (270 <= angDeg) & (angDeg < 360)
quad = 4;
else
error('input angle out of range')
end
angRad = angDeg * (pi/180);
sinAng = sin(angRad);
cosAng = cos(angRad);
Problem 8-A.6
Do the following tasks using loop structures:
1. Find the sum of the first 50 integers.
2. Find the product of the first 10 even integers.
Both of these problems should be straightforward. Both can be
done without a loop as well, using instead a vector operation.
It will help your students maintain continuity if you ask them
to produce a vector operation solution too. The code below shows
the equivalent vector operations as comments. Note that the
variables should *not* be named sum and prod because these are
names of MATLAB build-in functions.
% script for Prob8_A_6
clc, clear
% TASK 1: find sum of first 50 integers using a loop
my_sum = 0;
for i = 1:50
my_sum = my_sum + i;
end

[PAGE_233]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–227
my_sum
% solution with vector operation
% my_sum = sum(1:50)
% TASK 2: find product of first 10 integers using a loop
my_prod = 1;
for i = 2:2:10
my_prod = my_prod * i;
end
my_prod
% solution with vector operation
% my_prod = prod(1:10)
Problem 8-A.7
Consider the Form 4 IF-THEN-ELSE statement shown below and answer the
following questions. To get your answers, step through the code the way
MATLAB does when the code is executed. Keep track of the variables and
how their values change each time through the loop. It is easiest to do this if
you write down the variables and their values in a simple table. After you
have completed the paper and pencil exercise, perform this activity using
MATLAB, and verify your answers.
x=140;
for i = 1:10
if x < 1
break
elseif x>50
x=x/5;
elseif x>60
x=x/7;
else
x=x/10;
end
end
disp(x)
The built-in function break is serving a special purpose in the code above.
Look up break in MATLAB Help and be sure you understand how it is
being used here.

[PAGE_234]
8–228 More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming
1. What will MATLAB display?
2. How many times will this loop be executed?
The break statement executed inside a loop forces program con-
trol to “jump” to the outside of loop. In a sense, it enables a
way that the functionality of a standard WHILE loop can be
achieved by using a FOR loop.
To understand the code in this problem, students must get into
the mindset of “pretending to be the computer.” Loops are
typically hard for students to understand if they have not dealt
with them before. Even though each line of code may in isolation
be understood, still some students have difficulty imagining the
code repeating again and again. A large contributing factor is
that these students do not systematically step through the code
– again… pretending to be the computer.
Inserting the code lines as shown below will help students
understand the end result better as well.
% script for code of Prob 8-A.7
x=140;
for i = 1:10
i
if x < 1
break
elseif x>50
x=x/5;
elseif x>60
x=x/7;
else
x=x/10;
end
[i x]
end
disp(x)

[PAGE_235]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–229
Problem 8-A.8
Write a function, Prob8_A_8, that (a) returns the value of N such that the

1
series sum for ---- is precise to within 0.001 and (b) returns the value of the
2
k
k=1
series to that precision.
Since this infinite series is monotonically decreasing, you can determine when
to stop (and to stop using break) by comparing the sum after M terms have
been added, to the sum after M-1 terms have been added:
sum -sum < 0.001, then N = M.
M M-1
This problem is common when dealing with computing an infinite series. You
cannot set up your computational loop to do an infinite number of iterations
through the loop because that would require infinite time. The solution is to
determine a degree of precision that you will be content with, then stop the
loop and return the answer when that level of precision is reached.
This problem is meant as an exercise in FOR loop setup. The
important point for students to learn is that before a LOOP is
set up, it is sometimes (usually) necessary to define one or
more variables to maintain a “memory” of the results or settings
in earlier iterations.
A function meeting the specs for this problem is below.
function [stopAt_N, seriesSum] = Prob8_A_8
% Determines when to stop the series 1/k... such that the
% precision of the sum of the series is accurate to within
% 0.001. The value of the series sum to precision 0.001 is
% also returned.
% INPUTS: none
% OUTPUTS: stopAt_N, scalar, the number of the series
% element such that the series sum attains
% precision of 0.001.
% seriesSum, scalar, sum of the series to stopAt_N
% terms
% sample call:
% [stopAt_N, seriesSum] = Prob8_A_8
seriesSum = 0;
oldTerm = 1;
for i = 2:inf
newTerm = 1/(i^2);

[PAGE_236]
8–230 More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming
seriesSum = seriesSum + newTerm;
if (oldTerm - newTerm) <= 0.001
stopAt_N = i;
break
end
oldTerm = newTerm;
end

Problem 8-A.10
Write a MATLAB function, Prob8_A_10, that inputs two vectors and com-
putes and returns a vector that contains their cell-by-cell sum. Assume that
inputs are two same-length vectors, and use a FOR LOOP over columns to
compute the cell-by-cell sum.
function vSum = Prob8_A_10(v1, v2)
% computes the cell-by-cell sum of two vectors using a loop
% INPUT: v1, row vector of length M
% v2, row vector of length M
% OUTPUT: vSum, col vector of corresponding sums
% example call:
% vSum = Prob8_A_10([3, 1, 5], [2,10,3])
vSum = [];
for oneRow = [v1; v2]
vSum = [vSum; sum(oneRow)];
end
Problem 8-A.11
Write a MATLAB function, Prob8_A_11, that inputs two arrays and com-
putes and returns an array that contains their cell-by-cell sum. Assume that
inputs are two same-sized arrays, and use two nested FOR LOOP constructs
to compute the cell-by-cell sum.

[PAGE_238]
8–232 More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming
This exercise is very important. Students should take away from
this problem the ability to “step through” the elements of an
array. One key point for students: that because an array is two
dimensional, two loops are required.
function aSum = Prob8_A_11(A1, A2)
% computes the cell-by-cell sum of two arrays
% using two nested loop
% INPUT: A1, array of size N x M
% A2, array of size N x M
% OUTPUT: aSum, array of size N x M
% of corresponding sums
% example call:
% A = [1 2 3; 4 5 6];
% B = [10 20 30; 40 50 60];
% mySum = Prob8_A_11(A, B)
[numRows, numCols] = size(A1);
for rCt = 1:numRows
for cCt = 1:numCols
aSum(rCt, cCt) = A1(rCt, cCt) + A2(rCt, cCt);
end
end
Problem 8-A.12
Consider this sequence:
3
7k k = 123 10
Your task is to compute the sum of the first ten elements of the series.
Write a function, Prob8_A_12a, that solves the problem using vector opera-
tions.
Write a second function, Prob8_A_12b, that solves the problem using a
FOR LOOP.
Be sure that your two functions are returning the same answer.
For this problem students create a vector solution as a solution
based on using a loop. Students need to be conversant with both

[PAGE_239]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–233
methods in order to be able to choose the most applicable method
for a given problem.
Solutions following both paths are shown below. Notice that the
solution using a FOR loop (Prob8_A_12b) goes beyond the problem
specification and allows an integer input to indicate how many
elements of the series to use in a sum. The extension is made
here largely to accommodate the next problem.
function sumFirstTen = Prob8_A_12a
% compute the sum of elements 7k^3, for k = 1,2,..., 10. Use
vector operations
% INPUTS: none
% OUTPUTS: sumFirstTen, scalar, the indicated sum
% sample call:
% mySum = Prob8_A_12a
kVals = 1:10;
sumFirstTen = sum(7*kVals.^3);
function sumFirstN = Prob8_A_12b(N)
% compute the sum of elements 7k^3, for k = 1,2,..., N.
% Use a FOR loop.
% INPUTS: N, positive integer, sum extends to Nth element
% OUTPUTS: sumFirstN, scalar, the indicated sum
% sample call:
% mySum = Prob8_A_12b(10)
sumFirstN = 0;
for k = 1:N
sumFirstN = sumFirstN + 7*k^3;
end
Problem 8-A.13
For the sequence given in problem A.12, write a function, Prob8_A_13, that
inputs a positive integer N and calculates and returns a vector containing the
first N series sums as a vector output. (Use the series in Problem A.12.) Use
Prob8_A_12b (the second function you wrote for Problem A.12) in your
new function, Prob8_A_13.
This problem cannot be solved as stated. The problem is that
Prob8_A_12b does is not described as having a input. Thus to

[PAGE_240]
8–234 More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming
solve this problem as specified, an extension to Prob8_A_12b
must be accomplished.
There are two distinct lessons for students here. First,
specifications should be well understood. Modifications to
specifications may be necessary. Second, once Prob8_A_12b is
correctly extended, this problem becomes easy; the second lesson
focuses on problem decomposition.
function seriesSumsV = Prob8_A_13(N)
% Computes the first N series sums for the series with
% elements 7k^3. For example, for N = 3, the output will
% have three elements [7, 7+7*2^3, 7+7*2^3+7*3^3], or
% [7, 63, 252].
% INPUTS: N, integer
% OUTPUTS: seriesSumsV, vector with N elements, each a
% series sum as described above
% sample call:
% sumsVector = Prob8_A_13(3)
% uses function:
% Prob8_A_12b
seriesSumsV = [];
for i = 1:N
seriesSumsV = [seriesSumsV, Prob8_A_12b(i)];
end

function currentMax = Prob8_A_14(valueVector)

for oneVal = valueVector;
if oneVal > currentMax

Problem 8-A.15
A student writes the following code as part of a function to calculate the
growth of $10,000 in a bank account over the next 25 years. He assumes a
constant interest rate of 6%:
initial_money=10000 % dollars
for year = 1:25
% new balance is found by applying the interest
money=(initial_money*1.06);
% increment year by one
year=year+1;
end
Put this code into a function that has no input and that returns money and call
it Prob8_A_15.
This code has two runtime errors. Find and fix the problems and test your
revised function in MATLAB.
A correct form of the code is shown below. Both runtime errors
have to do with removing the variable initial_money and replac-
ing it with the variable money. The line “year = year + 1”
placed as it was in the original code above had no effect - it
was a NOOP. Loop variables (year in the example) can be reset in
a loop, but (a) it will have no effect on the next iteration and
(b) it is bad programming form largely from an “understandabil-
ity” dimension.
function money = Prob8_A_15
% calculate growth in a bank account:
% deposits $10,000 at the start
% leaves the money in the account for 25 yrs
% gets a return of 6% every year
money = 10000; % dollars
for year = 1:25
% new balance is found by applying the interest
money=(money*1.06);

[PAGE_242]
8–236 More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming
end
Problem 8-A.16
George opens a new bank account with $35,000. He is planning to deposit
$5,000 every year for the next 14 years. The interest rate on his account is 4%
and is constant. George would like to create a plot showing the growth of his
money. He would like enough flexibility to see what would happen with dif-
fering values for (a) initial deposit, (b) annual deposits, (c) interest rates, and
(d) length of time he will maintain the account.
Create a function, Prob8_A_16. The function should input the four vari-
ables that George wants to vary, create a plot of the amount of money in
George’s account versus time, and output a vector where each element is the
starting yearly balance for the corresponding year.
This problem is conceptually not difficult. There is one detail
that many students tend to trip over: setting a starting point
of the savings account at year 0. Put another way, the value of
the savings account at the end of year 1 (or the start of year
2) should be one year after the initial deposit. In the solution
shown below, this detail is handled by building a vector,
yrBalance, of yearly balance values such that yrBalance(1) is
the initial deposit, yrBalance(2) is the value after 1 year, …,
and yrBalance(N+1) is the balance in the account after N years.
function yrBalance = Prob8_A_16(initDeposit, annualDeposit, ...
interestRate, time)
% Compute and plot the yearly beginning balance for a bank
% account.
% INPUTS: initDeposit, scalar, the amount initially
% deposited (dollars)
% annualDeposit, scalar, the amount deposited at the
% start of each year after the first
% (dollars)
% interestRate, scalar, constant interest rate
% (decimal)
% time, scalar, the number of years the account will
% be active
% OUTPUTS: yrBalance, vector with <time+1> elements showing
% the yearly starting balance
% first element is the initial deposit
% sample call:
% bal = Prob8_A_16(10000, 1000, 0.05, 30)
% also create a plot of yrBalance VS time

[PAGE_243]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–237
yrBalance(1) = initDeposit;
for yrNum = 2:time+1
yrBalance(yrNum) = ...
yrBalance(yrNum-1)* (1 + interestRate) + ...
annualDeposit;
end
figure
plot(0:time, yrBalance, 'LineWidth', 2)
grid on
title('Bank Account Balance vs. Time', 'FontSize', 14)
xlabel('number of years', 'FontWeight', 'bold')
ylabel('account balance (dollars)', 'FontWeight', 'bold')
annotationStr = sprintf(['init deposit = $%6.0f\n',...
'yearly deposit = $%6.0f\n',...
'interest rate = %2.1f percent\n',...
'time = %2.0f years'], ...
initDeposit, annualDeposit, ...
interestRate*100, time);
disp('PLACE AN ANNOTATION STRING')
gtext(annotationStr, 'FontWeight', 'bold')
.

Problem 8-B.1
The Freshest Foods Market is announcing a new promotion. Their theme is:
“Cutting prices as you buy! 10% off every additional
item you buy after your purchase totals $20, and 20%
off after $50!”
When she reaches the counter Julie arranges her groceries as follows:
Item Gum Orange Cereal Cheese Coffee Tomato Pizza Sushi Shrimp
Unit
0.45 2.95 1.99 4.99 4.95 3.99 7.99 16.95 11.99
Price ($)
Number
1 1 2 1 1 2 2 1 2
of items

[PAGE_244]
8–238 More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming
How much will Julie pay? Build a function, Prob8_B_1, to answer the ques-
tion. Input should be an array representing data as in the table above. Output
should be the total bill Julie will pay, as a scalar.
Use a FOR LOOP in your function. Consider how you would solve this prob-
lem with vector operations using the built-in function cumsum as part of your
solution.
Functions for both a FOR solution and a CUMSUM (vector) solution
are shown below.
Note the structure of the IF construct embedded in the FOR
solution. Some freshman learners will not distinguish in this
type of problem any necessity to put one or the other clause
first in the construct. As an exercise to emphasize this point,
the instructor might write the IF as the following ...
if totalCost > 20
discount = 0.10;
elseif totalCost > 50
discount = 0.20;
end
... and ask students to step through the code for cases of
totalCost = 21, and totalCost = 51, and indicate the value of
discount that results.
There is a more general lesson from this problem as well. Note
that the logic for the vector solution (shown below as
Prob8_B_1b) is somewhat more difficult to follow (and to write)
than the solution using a FOR construct (shown below as
Prob8_B_1). A very useful discussion can be carried on focusing
on the question “What makes this problem easier to do using a
FOR as opposed to vector operations?” The answer lies in the
nature of vector operations. It is very easy with vector
operations to operate on an entire vector or array at once. But
in this problem, the problem spec forces an identification of
the element in the array at which a certain condition is met.
This characteristic, although not full proof, is a good rule of
thumb to indicate that a FOR solution will be easier to devise
than a vector based solution.
function totalCost = Prob8_B_1(costAndNum_table)
% Compute the total cost of a grocery basket full of items
% when the following sale terms apply: after $20, there is a
% 10% discount; after $50, there is a 20% discount. Solve
% using a LOOP.
% INPUT: costAndNum_table, array, table in which each column
% is ... <unitPrice; numItemsBought>

[PAGE_245]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–239
% Note that the order is
% significant; sale is
% accomplished from low numbered
% columns to high numbered
% columns.
% OUTPUT: totalCost, scalar, the total cost of the
% groceries.
% sample call:
% dataTable = ...
% [0.45,2.95,1.99,4.99,4.95,3.99,7.99,16.95,11.99; ...
% 1, 1, 2, 1, 1, 2, 2, 1, 2];
% totCost = Prob8_B_1(dataTable)
count = 1;
totalCost = 0;
discount = 0.0;
for oneItem = costAndNum_table
oneItemPrice = oneItem(1);
oneItemNum = oneItem(2);
costThisItemNoDiscount = oneItemPrice * oneItemNum;
costThisItem = costThisItemNoDiscount * (1 - discount);
totalCost = totalCost + costThisItem;
if totalCost > 50
discount = 0.20;
elseif totalCost > 20
discount = 0.10;
end
end
function costAll = Prob8_B_1b(costAndNum_A)
% Compute the total cost of a grocery basket full of items
% when the following sale terms apply: after $20, there is a
% 10% discount; after $50, there is a 20% discount. Solve
% using vector operations.
% INPUT: costAndNum_A, array, table in which each column
% is ... <unitPrice; numItemsBought>
% Note that the order is significant;
% sale is accomplished from low
% numbered columns to high numbered
% columns.
% OUTPUT: totalCost, scalar, the total cost of the
% groceries.
% sample call:
% dataTable = ...
% [0.45,2.95,1.99,4.99,4.95,3.99,7.99,16.95,11.99; ...
% 1, 1, 2, 1, 1, 2, 2, 1, 2];
% totCost = Prob8_B_1b(dataTable)
origCost = costAndNum_A(1,:) .* costAndNum_A(2,:);
cost = origCost;
totalCost = cumsum(cost);
costAll = totalCost(end);

[PAGE_246]
8–240 More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming
cost_G20_ndx = find(totalCost > 20);
if length(cost_G20_ndx) <2
return
end
cost_G20_ndx(1) = [];
cost(cost_G20_ndx) = cost(cost_G20_ndx)*0.9;
totalCost = cumsum(cost);
costAll = totalCost(end);
cost_G50_ndx = find(totalCost > 50);
if length(cost_G50_ndx) <2
return
end
cost_G50_ndx(1) = [];
cost(cost_G50_ndx) = origCost(cost_G50_ndx)*0.8;
totalCost = cumsum(cost);
costAll = totalCost(end);
Problem 8-B.2
David deposits $1,000 at Bank A and Mark deposits $1,000 at Bank B. Bank
A offers a 5% annual interest rate whereas Bank B offers 8.5%.
Write a MATLAB function, Prob8_B_2, that inputs David’s initial deposit,
Mark’s initial deposit, the interest rate (as a percentage) offered by David’s
bank, and the interest rate (as a percentage) offered by Mark’s bank.
Your function should return the number of years for Mark’s savings to become
at least twice as much as David’s.
Assume that the annual interest payments are deposited to the accounts at the
end of each year, and that David and Mark do not deposit any additional
money after their initial deposit.
This exercise is relatively straightforward. Students may need
to be prompted for the use of inf and return embedded in an IF.
function yearNum = ...
Prob8_B_2(davidDep, markDep, davidIntRate, markIntRate)
% Compute the number of years for one bank account to be at
% least twice another. Only an initial deposit - no annual
% deposit. Simple interest earned at year end.
% INPUT: davidDep, scalar, amount of deposit of David
% markDep, scalar, amount of deposit of Mark
% davidIntRate, scalar, rate David earns (as percent)

[PAGE_247]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–241
% markIntRate, scalar, rate Mark earns (as percent)
% davidIntRate < markIntRate.
% OUTPUT: numYrs, scalar, number of years at which David's
% account balance is at least twice
% that of Mark's.
% sample call:
% yrsToDouble = Prob8_B_2(1000, 1000, 5, 8.5)
davidIntRate = davidIntRate / 100;
markIntRate = markIntRate / 100;
davidSum = davidDep;
markSum = markDep;
for yearNum = 1:inf
davidSum = davidSum * (1 + davidIntRate);
markSum = markSum * (1 + markIntRate);
if markSum > 2 * davidSum
return
end
end
Problem 8-B.3
This following code line will generate a vector of 100 random numbers in the
range [-0.5, 0.5]. (Look up rand in MATLAB Help.)
My_array = rand([1 100])-0.5;
Build a function, Prob8_B_3, that inputs a vector of 100 random numbers
between -0.5 to 0.5. Your function should scan the input vector from begin-
ning to end and count the number of times the sign changes moving from one
element to the next. Consider zero to be neither positive nor negative. Hence,
there are three number ranges to consider: less than zero, zero, and greater
than zero.
You might find it useful to remind yourself of the answers to the following
two questions: When is the product of two numbers negative? When is the
product zero?
If students work the problem without taking the “hint” it will
be a bit longer than if they do take the “hint.” There are three
states that elements of the input can be in: negative, positive,
or zero. There are transitions possible from
negative to zero,
negative to positive,
zero to negative,

[PAGE_248]
8–242 More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming
zero to positive,
positive to zero, and
positive to negative.
Thus to implement this directly would require a IF construct
with a total of six clauses. Taking the “hint” given in the
problem however makes the solution a good deal more compact, as
shown in the function below. But note in the solution given the
role that the second line of the ELSEIF clause plays: to block
the possibility of there being two successive element which are
both zero.
function numSignChanges = Prob8_B_3(rand100)
% Count the number of sign changes in the input vector.
% Elements in the input can be negative, zero, or positive.
% INPUT: rand100, vector, a vector of numbers
% OUTPUT: numSignChanges, scalar, the number of times the
% sign changes from element
% to the next in the input
% vector.
% sample call:
% randNums = rand([1 100])-0.5;
% numSignChanges = Prob8_B_3(rand100)
numSignChanges = 0;
previousNumber = rand100(1);
for thisNumber = rand100(2:end)
if previousNumber*thisNumber < 0
numSignChanges = numSignChanges + 1;
elseif (previousNumber*thisNumber == 0) & ...
((previousNumber ~=0) | (thisNumber ~=0))
numSignChanges = numSignChanges + 1;
end
previousNumber = thisNumber;
end
Problem 8-B.4
Write a game function, Prob8_B_4, that randomly chooses an integer
between 0 and 100 and asks the user to guess the number. The program should
prompt “Guess the number!” until the user enters the correct number or a
number outside of the range. If the guess is correct, the program displays
“Good guess!” and quits. If not, the program prompts “Guess higher” or
“Guess lower” according to the number guessed.
When the program terminates, the program displays the number of attempts
made by the user.

[PAGE_249]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–243
Use the built-in function input to get input from the player. (See MATLAB
Help.)
An interesting question to pose to students after they have a
working solution to this problem is … what is the best strategy
for a user to play the “game?” A discussion of search can fol-
lowing, ending with rough ideas of binary search.
If the user guess correctly the first time, or if the user
terminates the first time through the loop, the message
displayed by the function below is not really grammatical. An
exercise extending the problem would be to correct that
situation.
function Prob8_B_4
% An interactive game. Chooses a random number between 0 and
% 100 and asks the user to guess it.
% INPUT: none
% OUTPUT: none
% sample call:
% Prob8_B_4
theAnswer = round(rand*100);
disp('------------')
guessStr = sprintf([' GUESS AN INTEGER BETWEEN [0 100]\n',...
' (input -99 to terminate) >> ']);
guess = input(guessStr);
numberTries = 1;
for i = 1:inf
if theAnswer == guess
disp([' CORRECT! (after ', ...
num2str(numberTries), ' attempts)']);
disp('------------')
return
elseif (guess < 0) | (guess > 100)
disp([' TERMINATING! (after ', ...
num2str(numberTries), ' attempts)']);
disp('------------')
return
elseif theAnswer > guess
guess = input(' GUESS HIGHER >> ');
numberTries = numberTries + 1;
else
guess = input(' GUESS LOWER >> ');
numberTries = numberTries + 1;
end
end

Problem 8-B.5
The Fibonacci numbers are defined by the sequence such that each value is the
sum of the previous two values. The first two numbers in the Fibonacci
sequence are given below:
F = 1
1
F = 1
2
The formula for calculating the successive numbers in the sequence is the fol-
lowing:
F = F +F
i i–1 i–2
i is an integer greater than 2.
Write a function, Prob8_B_5, that takes one argument—a positive integer
N—and outputs a vector of Fibonacci values so the last number in the
sequence is the greatest Fibonacci number that is less than N.
An interesting extension to this problem is to ask students how
many elements their answer vector has when the input is 23,000.
Then ask them to estimate how many elements the answer vector
has for input of 23,000,000. And finally, of course, have them
run their answer function using input of 23,000,000. The answer
for 23,000 is 22 elements; the answer for 23,000,000 is 36 ele-
ments.
A function meeting the problem spec is below.
function fibVector = Prob8_B_5(N)
% Computes a set of Fibonacci numbers such that the last
% element in the sequence is the largest Fibonacci number
% less than the input integer N. (N >1).
% INPUT: N, scalar, a positive integer
% OUTPUT: fibVector, vector, Fibonacci sequence with last
% element the large Fibonacci
% number less than N.
% sample call:
% fibSeq = Prob8_B_5(22)
fibVector(1) = 1;
fibVector(2) = 1;
for i = 3:inf
fibVector(i) = fibVector(i-1) + fibVector(i-2);
if fibVector(i) >= N

[PAGE_251]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–245
fibVector(i) = [];
return
end
end
Problem 8-B.6
Your computer science professor gives you an extra credit project. Your job is
to write a function, Prob8_B_6, that determines the end-of-term grade for
each student in the course. The input to your function will be an array of stu-
dent grades. The professor also gives you the following sample input:
Student Term
Homework Midterm Final
ID Project
39 61 73 85 64
271 95 79 80 100
418 72 65 94 81
665 83 86 82 92
860 25 40 93 85
All grades will be weighted equally in determining the final grade:
homework+midterm+termpProj+final
overallGrade = ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
4
The professor promises the students to take individual progress into account at
the end of the term. If the term project grade is at least 15 points higher than
the homework grade, and the final grade is at least 10 points higher than the
midterm grade, you must calculate the overall grade by ignoring their home-
work and midterm scores:
termpProj+final
overallGrade = ----------------------------------------------
2
Your function takes an N x 5 array and outputs an N x 2 array. The first and
second columns in the output should be the student ID and end-of-term grade,
respectively. N is a variable, i.e., your function should work for any number of
students.

[PAGE_252]
8–246 More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming
The solution below uses the MATLAB capability to iterate over
entire columns of an array. When processing data arrays in which
an entire row or column is one “observation,” this capability
can be useful.
A solution for this problem is below.
function termGrades = Prob8_B_6(studentGrades)
% Compute term grades for a group of students. Term grade
% is average of grades in homework, midterms, term
% project, and final exam. If term project is at least 15
% pts better than homework, AND the final exam is at least
% 10 pts better than the midterm, THEN ignore the homework
% and midterm scores.
% INPUT: studentGrades, an N x 5 array where each row is
% <ID> <homework> <midterm> <term proj> <final exam>
% OUTPUT: termGrades, an N x 2 array where each row is
% <ID> <term grade>
% sample call:
% grades = [ 39, 61, 73, 85, 64;
% 271, 95, 79, 80, 100;
% 418, 72, 65, 94, 81;
% 665, 83, 86, 82, 92;
% 860, 25, 40, 93, 85];
% termGrade = Prob8_B_6(grades)
termGrades = [];
for oneGradeSet = studentGrades'
if (oneGradeSet(4) >= 15 + oneGradeSet(2)) & ...
(oneGradeSet(5) >= 10 + oneGradeSet(3))
oneTermGrade = (oneGradeSet(4) + oneGradeSet(5))/2;
else
oneTermGrade = sum(oneGradeSet(2:end))/4;
end
termGrades = [termGrades; oneGradeSet(1), oneTermGrade];
end
Problem 8-B.7
A mathematical sequence, f, is defined this way:
fn = fn–1+fn–1fn–2 for n > 2,
f(1) = 1
f(2) = 1

[PAGE_253]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–247
Write a function, Prob8_B_7, that inputs a positive integer N as input and
outputs the first N numbers in this sequence as a vector.
Test your function with the following:
• For an input of 1, the output should be 1
• For an input of 2, the output should be [1 1]
• For an input of 3, the output should be [1 1 2]
• For an input of 4, the output should be [1 1 2 4]
A function meeting the code specs for this problem is below.
function f = Prob8_B_7(N)
% Create the first N terms in a sequence defined by
% f(1) = 1, f(2) = 1,
% f(n) = f(n-1) + f(n-1)* f(n-2) for n>2
% INPUT: N, scalar, a positive integer
% OUTPUT: seqVec, vector, first N terms in the sequence
% defined above
% sample call:
% sequence = Prob8_B_7(5)
if N == 1
f(1) = 1;
elseif N ==2
f(1) = 1; f(2) = 1;
else
f(1) = 1; f(2) = 1;
for i = 3:inf
if i > N
return
else
f(i) = f(i-1) + f(i-1)* f(i-2);
end
end
end
Problem 8-B.8
The half-life of a radioisotope is the time required for half of the radioactive
mass in any sample to undergo radioactive decay. In a radioactive sample,
after one half-life, half of the original sample remains. After three half-lives,
one-eighth of the original mass remains.

[PAGE_254]
8–248 More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming
Write a MATLAB function, Prob8_B_8, that determines how many years it
would take for N atoms of radioactive material, with half-life of HL years, to
be reduced to a level at or below the target threshold of T atoms. (Normally,
we would use the mass of the radioactive material, but here we will use the
number of atoms.)
This function will have three inputs:
1. The total number of radioactive atoms we start with (inte-
ger)
2. The length of the half-life (in years)
3. The target threshold number of atoms (integer)
It will have two outputs:
1. numYears—The total number of years it will take for
the radioactive material to decay to a level at or below the
target threshold
2. atomsLeft—The number of atoms left after
numYears
One assumption is made in the solution below that is not obvi-
ous: the number half-lives will be an integer. It would be a
reasonable exercise to ask students to write down all assump-
tions they make after they have a working solution to the prob-
lem.
A function meeting the specs of this problem is below.
function [yrs, atomsLeft] = ...
Prob8_B_8(startAtoms, halfLife, tarAtoms)
% Computer the number of years for a radioactive sample to
% decay to a target level of radiation. Assumes that the
% answer will be in terms of a whole number of half lives.
% INPUT: startAtoms, scalar, number of atoms at time = 0 yrs
% halfLife, scalar, number of yrs to halve the number
% of atoms
% tarAtoms, scalar, number of atoms we must be either
% at or below
% OUTPUT: yrs, scalar, number of years to reach tar Atoms or
% below
% atomsLeft, scalar, number of atoms left are yrs
% sample call:
% [Y, atoms] = Prob8_B_8(10^23, 2*10^2, 10^4)
atomsLeft = startAtoms;
for halfLifeCounter = 1:inf
atomsLeft = .5 * atomsLeft;
if atomsLeft <= tarAtoms

[PAGE_255]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–249
yrs = halfLifeCounter * halfLife;
return
end
end
Problem 8-B.9
As the ecological supervisor for the Great Hitonkin Forest, you are contracted
by a lumber company to find out the number of trees it will be allowed to cut
down over the upcoming years. The number of trees a lumber company is
allowed to chop in a given year can be modeled as 0.5 times the number of
seedlings planted the previous year minus 0.1 times the number of trees
chopped down the previous year.
The company provides you with the following data on the number of seed-
lings it will plant over the next 10 years:
seedlingPlanted = ...
[2700, 3000, 3000, 3200, 4000, 3700, 3300, 4200, 4400, 4100]
The lumber company tells you it plans to chop down 3,000 trees this year.
Write a MATLAB function, Prob8_B_9, that inputs the following:
• The number of trees chopped down in the first year
• A vector for the number of seedlings that will be planted
in the next ten years
Your function should plot the projected number of trees the lumber company
is allowed to chop down in the next 10 years. (Be sure to label your plot well
because it will be part of your report to the lumber company.)
This problem is a straightforward inventory simulation problem.
The main hurdle for students is to take the “word description”
given in the problem spec and turn it into the core simulation
line: cuttings(yr) = 0.5*plantingV(yr-1) - 0.1*cuttings(yr-1).
The solution below creates a bar graph, although a line plot is
included as lines that are “commented out.” The reason that a
bar chart is preferable is that the cutting activity extends
over an entire year rather than happening at a given time
instant. Thus the bar chart is a more accurate reflection of the
situation being modeled, and thus a better way to transfer the
information on cuttings.

[PAGE_256]
8–250 More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming
Most students will produce a line plot to meet the specification
for this problem. A discussion on the fundamental purpose of
graphs (to convey information) can ensue, and lead to the bar
chart as the best vehicle for this problem.
A function meeting the specification for this problem is below.
function Prob8_B_9(treesCutYr1, planting)
% Computes the number of trees to be harvested in the next
% 10 years in the Hitonkin Forest. Relation for cuttings is
% cutting(YR) = 0.5*planting(YR-1) - 0.1*cutting(YR-1)
% INPUT: treesCutYr1, scalar, number of trees cut in Yr 1
% plantingV, vector, 10 elements, number of trees
% plants in each of next 10 years.
% OUTPUT: none
% generates a graph of number of trees to harvest vs time
% sample call:
% yr1_cut = 3000;
% plantings = 10^3*[2.7,3,3,3.2,4,3.7,3.3,4.2,4.4,4.1];
% Prob8_B_9(yr1_cut, plantings)
cuttings(1) = treesCutYr1;
for yr = 2:10;
cuttings(yr) = 0.5*plantingV(yr-1) - 0.1*cuttings(yr-1);
end
figure
% plot(1:10, cuttings, ...
% '--ro','LineWidth',2,...
% 'MarkerEdgeColor','k',...
% 'MarkerFaceColor','g',...
% 'MarkerSize',10)
bar(0.5:1:9.5, cuttings)
grid on
xlabel('year number', 'FontWeight', 'bold')
ylabel('number of trees to harvest')
title('Number of Trees to Harvest vs. Year Number', ...
'FontSize', 14)
Problem 8-B.10
Elaine opens a savings account with a 6% interest rate and deposits $10,000.
In the next 30 years, Elaine does the following:
1. She deposits $10,000 at the beginning of every year.

[PAGE_257]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–251
2. In years when she has more than $100,000, she deposits
$10,000 and then withdraws 10% of her total balance to
buy mutual funds.
Write a MATLAB function, Prob8_B_10, that has no inputs or outputs. The
function should plot Elaine’s account balance over the next 30 years. Do not
include the value of her mutual funds.
This is another bank account problem.
The problem specification is left purposefully unclear on one
point: whether at the start of each year the deposit of $10,000
is made first, or the test if the current balance is over
$100,000 is made first. On balance the spec seems to indicate
the former. But students should be encouraged to note this kind
of looseness in a problem specification, and encouraged further
to seek clarification.
An interesting extension to this problem is to ask students to
characterize the plot they create. In fact, you cannot see
enough “data” to make that characterization definitively.
However, if the number of years is changed to 100, the nature of
this model because clear: there is an approach to an asymptotic
value.
A function meeting the specs of the problem is below.
function Prob8_B_10
% plots value of bank account with:
% 6% interest rate
% $10^4 initial deposit
% time is over 30 yrs
% rule for deposit/withdrawal...
% deposit 10^4 start of each year
% if bal > $10^5,
% withdraw 10% balance to invest
% INPUTS: none
% OUTPUTS: none
% generates a graph per above
% NOTE: plot not to include value of mutual funds
% sample call:
% Prob8_B_10
numYrs = 30;
intRate = .06;
yearlyDeposit = 10^4;
startBal(1) = yearlyDeposit;
for yrNum = 2:numYrs+1
startBal(yrNum) = startBal(yrNum-1)*(1+intRate);

[PAGE_258]
8–252 More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming
startBal(yrNum) = startBal(yrNum) + yearlyDeposit;
if startBal(yrNum) > 10^5
startBal(yrNum) = startBal(yrNum)*0.9;
end
end
figure
plot(0:numYrs, startBal, '--ro', ...
'LineWidth',2,...
'MarkerEdgeColor','k',...
'MarkerFaceColor','g',...
'MarkerSize',6)
grid on

ylabel('account balance ($)', 'FontWeight', 'bold')

'FontWeight', 'bold')
Problem 8-B.11
The Registrar’s Office wants you to project the number of graduating students
in the upcoming years. The total number of graduating students can be mod-
eled this way:
The number of senior students who did not fulfill their requirements
in the previous year (we are going to assume that they finish in one
additional year)
PLUS
The number of new enrollments in senior class in the current year
MINUS
The number of senior students who did not fulfill their requirements
in the current year.
The Registrar’s Office gives you the projected new enrollment in the senior
class and the projected number of students that will not fulfill their require-
ments. This projected data are for each of the next 15 years:
projectedEnrollment = ...
[95, 81, 76, 88, 96, 121, 133, 112, 91, 89, 79, 74, 91, 121, 75]

[PAGE_259]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–253
projectedStudentsNotFulfilling = ...
[5, 6, 3, 10, 7, 4, 6, 8, 8, 3, 4, 3, 2, 10, 8]
The Registrar indicates that a total of 90 students graduated at the end of the
first year. Write a MATLAB function, Prob8_B_11, that inputs the
studentsGraduatingYear1, the projectedEnrollment, and the
projectedStudentsNotFulfilling. It should have no output vari-
ables. Your function should plot the projected number of students graduating
over the next 15 years.
This problem is similar to B.9 above. In the abstract, both can
be thought of as inventory problems. Although not the focus of
this problem, note that the function below is a solution going
beyond the problem specification in that the function will per-
form correctly on any number of years - provided the two vector
inputs are appropriate: the number of elements in each of the
input vectors is equal to the number of years over which the
simulation is to be run.
function Prob8_B_11(studentsGrad_yr1, projEnrollV, projNotFulV)
% create a plot of modeling results for students graduating
% over the next 15 years. Modeling relation is as follows.
% numGrad(YR) = projNotFulfill(YR-1) ...
% + projEnrollV(YR) ...
% - projNotFulV(YR)
% INPUT: studentsGrad_yr1, scalar, number of Yr1 graduates
% projEnrollV, vector, N elements, the enrollment
% projected for the senior class
% projNotFulV, vector, N elements, number of
% students not meeting requirements
% generates a graph of number graduated for N years.
% sample call:
% yr1_grads = 90;
% enrollment = [95, 81, 76, 88, 96, 121, 133, ...
% 112, 91, 89, 79, 74, 91, 121, 75];
% notFulfilling = [5, 6, 3, 10, 7, 4, 6, 8, 8, ...
% 3, 4, 3, 2, 10, 8];
% Prob8_B_11(yr1_grads, enrollment, notFulfilling)
numGrad(1) = studentsGrad_yr1;
numYears = length(projEnrollV);
for yrCounter = 2:numYears
numGrad(yrCounter) = projNotFulV(yrCounter - 1) ...
+ projEnrollV(yrCounter) ...
- projNotFulV(yrCounter);
end
figure
plot(1:numYears, numGrad, ':ro', ...
'LineWidth',2,...

Problem 8-B.12
Your engineering expertise is requested by the Vandelay Truck Company.
Truck prices are projected to increase next year, so Vandelay would like to
keep at least 1,000 trucks in the warehouse until the prices go up. The capac-
ity of the warehouse is 1,500 trucks, never to be exceeded. The following
model for warehouse management has been developed:
• The production schedule for the next twelve months is
[130 90 105 152 174 146 168 194 203 185
164 191]. All manufactured trucks will be stored in
the warehouse until they are sold.
• Vandelay is planning to sell 15% of inventory once more
than 1,050 trucks are in the warehouse. Otherwise, no
trucks will be sold.
• Current inventory is 850 trucks.
Vandelay would like to know if inventory will fall below 1,000 or exceed
1,500. Create a plot of inventory over the next 13 months.
For quick and easy plot generation, write a MATLAB function,
Prob8_B_12. Your function should input the percentage of trucks that will
be sold (15% in this case) when inventory goes over 1,050, and output a vec-
tor of inventories. It should plot a graph of inventory for the next 13 months.
Assume that the trucks produced in one month are transferred to the ware-
house at the beginning of the next month. Start with a beginning inventory of
850 and run your simulation from month 2 to month 13.
Hint: 15% of inventory will not always be an integer. Use built-in function
round to round the numbers to nearest integer.

[PAGE_261]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–255
This problem on the surface is straight forward. A function that
meets the problem specification is below. The function goes
beyond the problem spec per se by making a subplot of both the
inventory of Vandelay Trucks and the sales number (number of
trucks that may be sold) of Vandelay Trucks. An instructor ini-
tiated discussion might be held on the subject of what a user
really “needs” versus what a user has written in a problem spec-
ification. Note that in this problem, it might well be the case
that management of Vandelay Trucks will be interested in how
many cars they may sell in a given month. Note also that upper
and lower bounds on the inventory as set in the problem are also

function inventoryV = Prob8_B_12(percentTrucksSell)
% Computes the month by month starting inventory for
% Vandelay Truck by doing a simulation. Strategy for selling
% is ... SELL 15% of trucks if inventory is more than 1050

% selling trucks
% INPUT: percentTrucksSell, scalar, threshold for triggering
% sales - initially 15%
% OUTPUT: inventoryV, 13 element vector, the starting
% inventory for each of the next 13 months,
% with the current month being numbered 1
% creates a plot for inventoryV
% sample call:
% inventory = Prob8_B_12(0.15)

% SET INTERNAL CONSTANTS
startingInventory = 850;
productionSchedule = [130 90 105 152 174 146 168 ...
194 203 185 164 191];
thresholdForSale = 1050;
lenSim = length(productionSchedule)+1;
lowerBound = 1000;
upperBound = 1500;
% SET UP AND PERFORM THE SIMULATION
inventoryV(1) = startingInventory;
for month = 2:lenSim
onHand = inventoryV(month-1) ...
+ productionSchedule(month-1);
if onHand >thresholdForSale
sellNumber(month) = percentTrucksSell * onHand;
sellNumber(month) = round(sellNumber(month));
else
sellNumber(month) = 0;
end
inventoryV(month) = onHand - sellNumber(month);
end
figure
subplot(2,1,1)
plot(1:lenSim, inventoryV, '--bo', ...
'LineWidth',2,...
'MarkerEdgeColor','k',...
'MarkerFaceColor','g',...
'MarkerSize',10)
hold on

'LineWidth', 2)
plot(1:lenSim,upperBound*ones(1,lenSim), '-r',...
'LineWidth', 2)
title('Vandelay Trucks Projected Inventory & Sales',...
'FontSize', 14)
ylabel('monthly starting inventory', ...
'FontWeight', 'bold')

num2str(percentTrucksSell*100), '%'];

Problem 8-B.13
Dr. Crane opens a bank account with an initial deposit of $2,125. He will
receive 6.25% interest, compounded quarterly. Right after he deposits the
money, he moves to Paris and stays there for 19 years. He does not make any
deposits or withdrawals during this time.
Nineteen years later he returns home and goes to collect the money from his
account. How many dollars in interest has his account earned over the last 19
years? (Interest is compounded four times every year, which you can assume
means that for every three months, 6.25%/4 interest is paid to Dr. Crane’s
account.)
Write a Matlab function that will take the initial deposit, number of years,
and the interest rate (in percentage) as inputs. The only out-put will be the
interest he earned over the years he was gone.
A function meeting this problem specification is below.
function interestEarned = ...
Prob8_B_13(initDep, numYrs, interestRatePerCent)
% computes interest earned for a bank account with an
% initial deposit but now further deposits, over a set
% number of years, and with a set annual interest rate
% compounded quarterly.
% INPUT: initDep, scalar, $ of the initial deposit
% numYrs, scalar, the number of year the account "sits"
% interestRatePerCent, scalar, annual interest rate
% OUPPUT: interestEarned, scalar, the total amount of money
% earned over numYrs
% sample call:
% earned = Prob8_B_13(2125, 19, 6.25)
threeMonthIntRate = interestRatePerCent*.01/4;
interestEarned = 0;
accountBal = initDep;
for quartNumber = 1:numYrs*4
interestThisQuarter = accountBal * threeMonthIntRate;
accountBal = accountBal + interestThisQuarter;
interestEarned = interestEarned + interestThisQuarter;

[PAGE_265]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–259
end

The electric motor rotates the lever at a rate of 3 degrees/sec. The door rotates
freely (W = 0) until t = 10 seconds. The child clings to the door from t = 10 to
k
t = 20 seconds. At t = 20 seconds, the child gets scared and lets go.
Write a MATLAB function, Prob8_B_14, that inputs the time the child first
grabs the door and the time he eventually releases it. Your function should use
a FOR LOOP to make subplots of the force applied by the lever versus time
and torque produced by the electric motor versus timefrom t=0 seconds to

This problem is relatively straightforward if the problem specs
are carefully followed.
Code for the problem as written is below. There is also a second
version of a solution below that uses vector operations rather
than a loop. A good additional exercise would be to require a
vector solution of students.
function Prob8_B_14(timeKidOn, timeKidOff)
% Creates subplots force and torque given the problem spec
% 8-B.14. This version uses a FOR loop.
% INPUT: timeKidOn, scalar, the elapsed time at which a child
% jumps on the end of the door being
% opened
% timeKidOn, scalar, the elapsed time at which a child
% lets go of the door
% OUTPUT: none
% creates a subplot of force VS time, and torgue VS time
% sample call:
% Prob8_B_14(10, 20)
% uses Prob8_B_14_graph
% set up internal constants
weightDoor = 225; % lbs
lengthDoor = 16; % feet
lengthLever = 7; % feet
weightChild = 65; % lbs
i = 0;
for time = linspace(0,60,1000)
i = i + 1;
t(i) = time;
beta = 3 * (pi/180) * time; % radians
if ~(timeKidOn <= time & time <= timeKidOff) % kid is off
force(i) = (0.5*weightDoor*lengthDoor*(cos(beta/2))^2 ) ...
/ (lengthLever*(1+cos(beta))); % force with no kid
else % kid is on
force(i) = ((0.5 * weightDoor * lengthDoor ...

[PAGE_267]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–261
+ weightChild * lengthDoor) ...
* (cos(beta/2))^2 ) ...
/ (lengthLever*(1+cos(beta)));
end
torque(i) = force(i) * lengthLever .* sin((pi-beta)/2);
end
Prob8_B_14_graph(t, force, torque)

function Prob8_B_14b(timeKidOn, timeKidOff)
% Creates subplots force and torgue given the problem spec
% 8-B.14. This version uses vector computations.
% INPUT: timeKidOn, scalar, the elapsed time at which a child
% jumps on the end of the door being
% opened
% timeKidOn, scalar, the elapsed time at which a child
% lets go of the door
% OUTPUT: none
% creates a subplot of force VS time, and torgue VS time
% sample call:
% Prob8_B_14b(10, 20)
% uses Prob8_B_14_graph
% set up internal constants
weightDoor = 225; % lbs
lengthDoor = 16; % feet
lengthLever = 7; % feet
weightChild = 65; % lbs

[PAGE_268]
8–262 More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming
time = linspace(0,60, 1000);
timeKidOff_ndx = find(time < timeKidOn | time > timeKidOff);
timeKidOn_ndx = find(timeKidOn <= time & time <= timeKidOff);
beta = 3 * (pi/180) * time; % radians
force = ( 0.5*weightDoor*lengthDoor*(cos(beta/2)).^2 ) ...
./ (lengthLever*(1+cos(beta))); % force with no kid
force(timeKidOn_ndx) = ...
((0.5*weightDoor*lengthDoor + weightChild*lengthDoor) ...
* (cos(beta(timeKidOn_ndx)/2)).^2 ) ...
./ (lengthLever*(1+cos(beta(timeKidOn_ndx))));
% force with kid on
torque = force * lengthLever .* sin((pi-beta)/2);

Problem 8-B.15
The bisection method is a simple way of finding roots of polynomials numeri-
cally. Bisection method starts with two guesses, x and x , such that:
1 2
fx 0
1
fx 0
2
In such a case, the “zero value” of the polynomial (i.e., the root), must lie
between x and x . To close in on the root, we calculate the average of x and
1 2 1
x :
2
x +x
x = ---1------------2-
3 2
and we evaluate the polynomial at x . According to the value of fx , we
3 3
make one of the following decisions:
• If fx  is greater than zero, the root must lie between x
3 1
and x , so we replace x with x .
3 2 3
• If fx  is less than zero, the root must lie between x and
3 2
x , so we replace x with x .
3 1 3
• If fx  is zero, x is a root, so we quit.
3 3
We continue the “average-replace” strategy until we find a root or the differ-
ence between x and x is below a tolerance.
1 2

[PAGE_269]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–263
Use the Bisection Method described above in a function, Prob8_B_15, to
find a root between x = 0 and x = 5 for the following polynomial. Set
the tolerance in your solution to 10-3.
4 3 2
fx = –x +4x +43x –58x–240
This problem will be the most difficult in this chapter for most
students. The difficulty is translating the algorithm given in
the problem spec into MATLAB code. The real hurdle lies in
understand the problem spec itself. Most students will need to
work this problem out with paper and pencil for a simple case
before they try to implement a solution in MATLAB. An additional
aid is to use ezplot to create a functional graph for the poly-
nomial specified:
>> eS = '-x^4 + 4*x^3 + 43*x^2 - 58*x -240';
>> ezplot(eS, [-6,9])
… to produce the following:
4 3 2
-x + 4 x + 43 x - 58 x -240
600
400
200
0
-200
-400
-600
-800
-6 -4 -2 0 2 4 6 8
x
Note that the four roots of the polynomial are x=8, x=3, x=-5,
and x=-2. Note also that the conditions necessary for the

[PAGE_270]
8–264 More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming
bisection method as stated in the problem spec are met: If F(x)
is the polynomial, then F(0) < 0, and F(5) > 0. Finally, note
that there is a root in the (0,5) interval. The bisection method
as given in the problem specification will find that root.
A set of two functions that meets the explicit problem
specification is below. One of the two, Prob8_B_15b, evaluates a
polynomial F(x) for a given value of x. The second, Prob8_B_15,
implements the bisection algorithm as specified.
The bisection method given in the problem is capable of finding
two of the four roots: x=3 and x=-5. The other two roots, x=8
and x=-2, cannot be found with the algorithm given; the
assumptions of the method given are not met.
A challenging extra credit problem would be to enhance the
bisection method as given in the problem specification so that
all roots would be potentially accessible to the extended
method.
The extension could distinguish three cases. If F(x) is the
polynomial, the lower limit on the interval is a, and the upper
limit on the interval is b, then the three cases are as follows:
1) F(a) < 0 AND F(b) > 0 … this is the case covered in the
algorithm stated in the problem specification. For
F(x) = -x^4 + 4*x^3 + 43*x^2 - 58*x -240
roots at x=3 and x=5 can be found under this case.
2) F(a) > 0 AND F(b) < 0 … this is the case that can cover the
two roots of the polynomial at x=8 and x=-2.
3) F(a) and F(b) are of the same sign … This means that a new
pair of boundaries a and b must be chosen. The simple solution
to this case is to “punt” – create an error as in the code
below. A more sophisticated handling would be to generate a new
guess for a and b.
MATLAB includes a rich set of built-in function that operate on
polynomials. Two of particular note here are “roots,” which
finds all roots of a given polynomial, and “polyval,” which
evaluates a polynomial F(X) at a given x.
Code for Prob8_B_15 and Prob8_B_15b is below.
function oneRoot = Prob8_B_15(polyCoeff, tolerance, bounds)
% Compute a single zero for a polynomial in an interval using a
% bisection method. The polynomial is represented by a vector of
% its coefficients (from is represented by a vector of its
% coefficients (from highest to lowest). Let F(x) be the
% polynomial. Assumption is that F(bounds(1) < 0, and
% F(bonuds(2) > 0.

[PAGE_271]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–265
% INPUTS polyCoeff, vector, polynomial coefficients from
% highest power to constant.
% tolerance, scalar, acceptable tolerance for
% identifying a root
% bounds, two element vector, lower and upper range
% bound
% OUTPUT: oneRoot, scalar, one root of the polynomial in the
% given range
% sample call:
% coef = [-1, 4, 43, -58, -240];
% limits = [0, 5];
% tol = 10^-3;
% aRoot = Prob8_B_15(coef, tol, limits)
x1 = bounds(1);
x2 = bounds(2);
x3 = mean([x1,x2]);
% make sure assumptions of method are realized
fLower = Prob8_B_15b(polyCoeff, x1);
fUpper = Prob8_B_15b(polyCoeff, x2);
if (fLower >= 0) | (fUpper <= 0)
error(' !!! assumptions of method not met')
end
for i = 1:inf
f_x1 = Prob8_B_15b(polyCoeff, x1);
f_x2 = Prob8_B_15b(polyCoeff, x2);
f_x3 = Prob8_B_15b(polyCoeff, x3);
[1,3,2; x1, x3, x2; f_x1 f_x3 f_x2]
if abs(x1-x2) < tolerance
oneRoot = x3;
return
elseif f_x3 == 0
oneRoot = x3;
return
elseif f_x3 > 0
x2 = x3;
elseif f_x3 < 0
x1 = x3;
end
x3 = mean([x1,x2]);
end
function valOfPoly = Prob8_B_15b(coef, x)
% evaluate a polynomial with known coefficients at a given
% point
% INPUT: coef, vector of coefficients from highest power to
% the coefficient for the constant term
% x, scalar, point at which the polynomial is
% to be evaluated

[PAGE_272]
8–266 More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming
% OUTPUT: valOfPoly, scalar, the computed evaluation of the
% polynomial
% sample call:
% coef = [-1, 4, 43, -58, -240];
% This represents a polynomial
% -x^4 + 4x^3 + 43x^2 -58x -240
% x = 2;
% valOfPoly = Prob8_B_15b(coef, x)
for i = length(coef):-1:1
powersX(i) = x^(i-1);
end
powerX = powersX(end:-1:1);
valOfPoly = sum(coef .* powerX);
Problem 8-B.16
A mass-spring system is set up as shown in the picture below:
MM
dd
xx
dd
dd
dd
dd
This arrangement shows 11 springs: one that touches the platform and 5 pairs
of springs with d meters difference in heights. When mass M is supported by
the platform, the platform will move through a distance x until the weight is
balanced by the force of some number of the springs. The number of springs
that it takes to balance the weight depends on the mass being supported by the
platform (M).
A spring exerts a force proportional to its compression. The constant of pro-
portionality is called the “spring constant.” For a single spring of spring con-
stant k, supporting a mass of M, the balance of forces (gravitational VS spring
compressional) is as follows, given g as the acceleration due to gravity, 9.81
m/s^2:

[PAGE_273]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–267
Mg = kx if xd
Mg
orx = --------
k
If the mass being supported requires both the center spring and the first spring
pair out from the center in order to support it, then the balance of forces gives
us:
Mg = kx+2kx–d if dx2d
Mg+2kd
orx = ------------------------
3k
Write a MATLAB function, Prob8_B_16_ver1, that inputs M, d, and k
and outputs the loaded position of the platform (x). Assume only three springs
are available: the center spring and the spring pair closest the center spring.
Try your Prob8_B_16_ver1 with inputs M=10 kg, d=0.2 m, and k=200 N/
m. (Your answer should be x=0.29667 m.)
Now, generalize your solution to N pairs of springs, with spring pair n=0 to
mean the single center spring, then count pairs of springs out from the center.
When spring pair n is supporting the platform, the loaded position of the plat-
form (x) can be calculated using:
Mg+nn+1kd
x = ----------------------------------------- ifndxn+1 d
2n+1k
Convince yourself that the above general relation is correct, and be prepared
to convince your instructor.
Notice that you have to know which region the platform comes to rest in
before you can compute its position. This seems circular: you have to know
the position to compute the position!
But think of solving the general problem as follows. Assume the platform
comes to rest in the region supported by only one spring, and compute the
loaded position. If the loaded position turns out to be greater than the region
of one spring only, then recompute with three springs. And continue until the
loaded position that you calculate meets the assumption for which region the
platform will be in when loaded.
Now, starting from your function Prob8_B_16_ver1, create a new function
Prob8_B_16_ver2 that takes inputs of N, M, d, and k and outputs (a) the
loaded position of the platform (x), and (b) the number of the spring pair the
last to engage in supporting the platform. Be careful to understand what N
stands for: the number of spring pairs in a system. Note again that N=0 repre-
sents the center spring.

[PAGE_274]
8–268 More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming
Try your function with inputs that you used before for version 1; the results
for version 1 should agree with the results for version 2.
Now try inputs of N=11, M=100 kg, d=0.2 m, k=200 N/m. (Your answers
should be x=1.9789 m, and springNumber=9.)
Most students will find this problem challenging. The main hur-
dle for students is to understand the physical situation and the
problem specification.
Functions implementing a solution for this problem follow.
function x = Prob8_B_16_ver1(M, d, k)
% For a set of 2N + 1 springs organized as show in the figure
% associated with the problem spec, determine the value of X for
% given values of (a) supported mass (M), (b) no-load end point
% separations of the spring pairs (d), and same spring constants
% for all springs (k)
% INPUT: M, scalar, the mass of the object supported (kg)
% d, scalar, the no-load distances between spring pairs
% k, scalar, the spring constant for all springs
% OUTPUT: x, scalar, the loaded position of the platform
% INTERNAL CONSTANT: g, scalar, accel due to gravity = 9.8 m/s^2
% sample call:
% equibilbriumHeight = Prob8_B_16_ver1(10, 0.2, 200)
% set local constants
g = 9.8; % m/s^2
solutionWithCenterSpringOnly = weight/k;
if solutionWithCenterSpringOnly <= d
x = solutionWithCenterSpringOnly;
else
x = (M*g + 2*k*d) / (3*k);
end
function [x, n] = Prob8_B_16_ver2(N, M, d, k)
% For a set of 2N + 1 springs organized as show in the figure
% associated with the problem spec, determine the value of X
% for given values of (a) supported mass (M), (b) no-load
% end point separations of the spring pairs (d), and same spring
% constants for all springs (k). Return as well the number of
% the spring pair last engaged to support the platform.
% INPUT: N, scalar, 0 or positive integer, num of spring pairs
% M, scalar, the mass of the object supported (kg)
% d, scalar, the no-load distances between spring pairs
% k, scalar, the spring constant for all springs
% OUTPUT: x, scalar, the loaded position of the platform

[PAGE_275]
More Flexibility: Introduction to Conditional and Iterative Programming 8–269
% n, scalar, the spring pair that is the last to engage
% INTERNAL CONSTANT: g, scalar, accel due to gravity = 9.8 m/s^2
% sample call:
% [loadedX, springPairNumber] = Prob8_B_16_ver2(11,100,0.2,50)
% set local constants
g = 9.8; % m/s^2
for n = 0:N
x = ( M*g + n*(n+1)*k*d ) / ( (2*n+1)*k );
if x <= (n+1)*d
return
end
end
